An Investigation on Kripke-style Modal Type Theories
Jason Z. S. Hu, Brigitte Pientka

Abstract
. This technical report investigates Kripke-style modal type theories, both simply typed and dependently typed. We examine basic meta-theories of the type theories, develop their substitution calculi, and give normalization by evaluation algorithms.



1.
Introduction

Modalities are a widely used concept. Certain modalities can readily be implemented in
existing languages, e.g. various (co)monad instances in Haskell
libraries. Unfortunately, not every modality can be implemented in well-known
languages and this problem triggers studies of adding various modalities into
different type
theories
(
Davies and
Pfenning 2001
;
Pfenning and
Davies 2001
;
Gratzer
et al. 2019
;
Birkedal et al. 2020
;
Kavvos 2017
;
Clouston 2018
;
Davies 2017
;
Nakano 2000
, etc)
.
Though modalities have been researched quite extensively in the logic communities,
some applications of modalities in programming languages have more specialized
scenarios which allow programmers to assume more specific structures in the modalities
than commonly known as logical systems. On the other hand,

Davies and
Pfenning 2001
;
Pfenning and
Davies 2001
;
Kavvos 2017
;
Clouston 2018

give completely general formulation in natural deduction styles that is complete with
respect to their corresponding modal logical systems.

1.1.
Modality by Axioms

Traditionally, necessity in modal logics is described by some combination of
axioms. Among all, necessity must satisfy the following axiom:

\displaystyle K:\square(A\to B)\to\square A\to\square B

The system satisfying this axiom only is also called
K
. In addition, we might admit
the following axiom:

\displaystyle T:\square A\to A

The system satisfying both
K
and
T
is called
T
. Another common axiom is

\displaystyle 4:\square A\to\square\square A

Admittedly it is strange that the name of an axiom is
4
, but
c’est la
vie
. If we extend system
T
with the axiom
4
, then we obtain
S4
. We call the
system which admits only axioms
K
and
4
system
K4
.
There are other axioms but we will not cover them
here. An experienced eye may identify that
K
effectively is the applicative functor
law and
T
and
4
combined are the comonad laws. Therefore,
S4
describes the
necessity modality as a comonad, so necessity in
S4
is sometimes called

a comonadic modality
and
S4
is in
a comonadic type theory
.

1.2.
Different Formulations

In this technical report, we consider the general formulations of necessity modality
in Kripke style (given as the implicit formulation in
Davies and
Pfenning 2001
). We
distinguish this style from another style, the dual-context or explicit style, given
in the same paper by how contexts are organized. The dual-context style is inspired by
the categorization of two different judgments for a proposition, validity or
truth. In
S4
, validity is a stronger assertion than truth; in particular, if a proposition is
valid, then it is also true, but not necessarily vice versa. This categorization of
propositions corresponds to two different contexts serving different purposes. In a
typing judgment in dual-context style, we have

\Delta\;|\;\Gamma\vdash t:T

where
\Delta
records valid assumption while
\Gamma
records the true ones. In
this view, the necessity modality, or
\square
, has the following introduction and
elimination rules in
S4
:

\frac{\Delta\;|\;\cdot\vdash t:T}{\Delta\;|\;\Gamma\vdash\texttt{box}\ t:\square T}\par\frac{\Delta\;|\;\Gamma\vdash t:\square T\quad\Delta,u:T\;|\;\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:T^{\prime}}{\Delta\;|\;\Gamma\vdash\texttt{letbox}\ {u}=t\ \texttt{in}\ t^{\prime}:T}

where
\square T
can be read as
T
being valid. In this reading, the
introduction rule says that
T
is valid if it can be derived only from valid
assumptions. The elimination rule allows use of
\square T
by pattern matching it and
appends a valid assumption to
\Delta
in the match body.

Kripke style, on the other hand, takes a different approach. It starts from Kripke
semantics from a logical point of view. Kripke semantics says that assuming an
accessibility relation via which different “worlds” are connected, we can model
various modal
logics by considering what structure this relation possesses:

•

If the accessibility relation has no particular structure, then we have system
K
.

•

If the relation is reflexive, then we have system
T
.

•

If the relation is both reflexive and transitive, then we have system
S4
.

•

If the relation is only transitive, not reflexive, then we have system
K4
.

Inspired by this interpretation, the calculus instead uses a stack of contexts
to keep track of assumptions in different worlds accessed through the relation, and
the structure of the relation is revealed in the form of elimination. All four aforementioned systems have
the following introduction and elimination rules for
\square
:

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\vdash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{box}\ t:\square T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:\square T\quad|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|=n}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t:T}

From Kripke semantic point of view, the introduction rule states that we can follow
the accessibility relation to a new world, in which no assumption is made. On the
other hand, the elimination rule allows us to travel backward to the
already accessed worlds by specifying a proper
unbox
level
n
. This
unbox
level

n
is a reflection of Kripke semantics. Its range decides which modal logic the
calculus corresponds to:

•

If
n=1
, then the system is
T
.
1
1

1

In this case, we could have omitted
the
unbox
level completely, but from a theoretical point of view, keeping the

unbox
level here helps greatly with uniformity.

•

If
n\in[0,1]
, then the system is
T
.

•

If
n\in\mathbb{N}
namely all natural numbers, then the system is
S4
.

•

If
n\in[1,+\infty)
namely all positive numbers, then the system is
K4
.

Notice that
1
is always in the range. This is because
n=1
corresponds to the
axiom
K
, which is the minimal axiom the modality should admit. In this report, we
will apply this observation very frequently.
The fact that the calculus can be parameterized over the range of
unbox
levels is
one major advantage of Kripke style over dual-context style. In dual-context style,
different modal logics have very different formulations, as shown in

Kavvos 2017
.

Another advantage of Kripke style is that
\square T
is considered as a product type,
as opposed to a sum type in dual-context style, where we have to handle commuting
conversions (c.f.
Girard 1989
;
Kavvos 2017
). In Kripke
style, this problem naturally goes away. These reasons lead to the choice of study in
this technical report.

1.3.
Fitch-style Systems

Some investigations effectively study the same group of systems under the name of “Fitch
style”. Kripke style and Fitch style are fundamentally the same and only differ
slightly by how they organize contexts. As shown above, Kripke style manages
context stacks. In Fitch style, a single context is maintained, but in the
context a delimiter symbol (usually

) is used. The segment of context
appearing after

is considered assumptions in a new world until the next

. For
K
, in Fitch style we
would have the following formulation:

\frac{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}\vdash t:T}{\Gamma\vdash t:\square T}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash t:\square T\quad\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}\notin\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}},\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\texttt{unbox}\ t:T}

Compared to the Kripke-style elimination rule of
K
,

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:\square T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vdash\texttt{unbox}\ t:T}

since the world structure is less clear in Fitch style, a predicate
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}\notin\Gamma^{\prime}
is added to ensure all assumptions in
\Gamma^{\prime}
are in the same world. This
is unnecessary in Kripke style as the world structure is baked in the syntax of
context stacks. One can
also imagine writing out the elimination rule of
S4
in Fitch style can also be
annoying as one must count the number of

“just right”. Therefore, in this
technical report, we use Kripke style in the syntax. However, as we will see in the
constructions later, the line between Kripke and Fitch styles becomes much more blur
in the semantics.

1.4.
Contributions

To summarize what has been achieved in this technical report, we

•

provide a general scheme for substitutions for all four aforementioned systems,
which generalizes well with dependent types;

•

provide two normalization by evaluation (NbE) algorithms, one based on a
presheaf model and the other based on an untyped domain, so that they work for all
four systems, and thus demonstrate their normalization at once;

•

define a substitution calculus, which, again, works for all four systems;

•

extend Martin-Löf type theory with modality and explicit substitutions, such
that the four systems have Martin-Löf style dependent types;

•

at last, adapt the untyped domain model to dependent types, so that we
obtain normalization for the dependently typed variants.

This technical report contains necessary discussion and proofs. A partial
formalization of certain concepts in Agda
2
2

2

primarily for simply types
can be found at

https://gitlab.com/JasonHuZS/practice
.

2.
Simply Typed Modal Type Theory

In this section, we introduce the syntax and judgments for simply typed modal type
theory as per
Davies and
Pfenning 2001
and its related operations, parameterized by
some unspecified base type
B
. Effectively, we extend simply typed
\lambda
-calculus
with
\square
modality shown in the previous section. We make the syntax precise below

(Types,
Typ
)

\displaystyle S,T

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ B\;|\;\square T\;|\;S\longrightarrow T

(Meta-level natural numbers,
\mathbb{N}
)

\displaystyle i,j,k,l,m,n

(variables,
Var
)

\displaystyle x,y

(Terms,
Exp
)

\displaystyle s,t,u

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ x

(box)

\displaystyle\;|\;\texttt{box}\ t\;|\;\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t

(functions)

\displaystyle\;|\;\lambda x.t\;|\;s\ t

(Contexts,
Ctx
)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Delta,\Phi

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \cdot\;|\;\Gamma,x:T

(Context
stack,
\overrightarrow{\textsf{Ctx}}
)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\Gamma},\overrightarrow{\Delta}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \epsilon\;|\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma

(Normal form (
Nf
))

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ v\;|\;\texttt{box}\ w\;|\;\lambda x.w

(Neutral form
(
Ne
))

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ x\;|\;v\ w\;|\;\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ v

B
represents the base type. In order to make our argument as general as possible, we
leave the naming convention abstract. We assume silent
\alpha
renaming whenever
necessary. We use
\epsilon
to represent the empty context stack and
\cdot
to
represent the empty context. For example,
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot
means to append an empty
context on top of the existing context stack
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
. We write
\epsilon;\cdot
for
the singleton stack containing the empty context. We overload
;
for combining two
context stacks as well. For example, we write
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Delta}
for a combined
context stack where
\overrightarrow{\Delta}
is appended on top of
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
.

2.1.
Typing Rules

The typing judgments for
Exp
are listed below:

\par\frac{x:T\in\Gamma}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vdash x:T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\vdash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{box}\ t:\square T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:\square T\quad|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|=n}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t:T}\par\\
\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma,x:S\vdash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t:S\longrightarrow T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:S\longrightarrow T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash s:S}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\ s:T}

A variable can only refer to bindings in the topmost context in the stack. The introduction
and elimination rules for
\square
are the same as in
Section

1
and by
changing the range of
n
we obtain different systems. The last two rules for
functions are standard. Moreover, we stipulate that a context stack being typed in
must have at least length
1
. That means we can always assume there exists a topmost context
which we are working in.

2.2.
Modal Transformation

In order to define equivalence between well-typed terms, we need to define a few
operations.
The
modal transformation
operation, or
MoT
, converts a term in one context stack to another by
re-index the
unbox
levels:

Definition 2.1.

MoT is defined as

\displaystyle x\{k/l\}

\displaystyle:=x

\displaystyle\texttt{box}\ t\{k/l\}

\displaystyle:=\texttt{box}\ (t\{k/l+1\})

\displaystyle\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t\{k/l\}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t\{k/l-n\}&\text{if $n\leq l$}\\
\texttt{unbox}_{k+n-1}\ t&\text{if $n>l$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\lambda x.t\{k/l\}

\displaystyle:=\lambda x.(t\{k/l\})

\displaystyle s\ t\{k/l\}

\displaystyle:=(s\{k/l\})\ (t\{k/l\})

In particular, we call the cases where
k=0

modal fusion
3
3

3

Since
k

comes from an
unbox
level, systems that do not admit the axiom
T
do not have
modal fusion. In the rest of this report, we will always assume that modal fusion is
possible. This will not affect the validity of the discussion for systems that do
not have modal fusion.
, and the rest
modal weakening
4
4

4

On the other
hand, since
k=1
is always possible, all systems have modal weakening.
. The
reason can be seen from the following lemma:

Lemma 2.2.

If
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0};\cdots;\Delta_{l}\vdash t:T
, then

\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{0};\cdots;(\Gamma_{k},\Delta_{0});\cdots;\Delta_{l}\vdash t\{k/l\}:T
.

Proof.

Induction on
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0};\cdots;\Delta_{l}\vdash t:T
.
∎

To understand this lemma, we should consider some special values.

•

When
k=l=0
, the lemma states that if
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0}\vdash t:T
, then

\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma_{0},\Delta_{0})\vdash t\{0/0\}:T
. Notice that the topmost two
contexts are fused into one, hence “modal fusion”.

•

When
k=2
and
l=0
, the lemma states that if
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0}\vdash t:T
, then

\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1};(\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{0})\vdash t\{2/0\}:T
. A new
context
\Gamma_{1}
is inserted to the stack and the topmost context is (locally)
weakened by
\Gamma_{2}
, hence “modal weakening”.

MoTs satisfy the following equations:

Lemma 2.3.

The following holds:

•

If
l^{\prime}<l
, then
t\{k/l\}\{k^{\prime}/l^{\prime}\}=t\{k^{\prime}/l^{\prime}\}\{k/l+k^{\prime}-1\}
.

•

If
l\leq l^{\prime}<l+k
, then
t\{k/l\}\{k^{\prime}/l^{\prime}\}=t\{k+k^{\prime}-1/l\}
.

•

If
l+k\leq l^{\prime}
, then
t\{k/l\}\{k^{\prime}/l^{\prime}\}=t\{k^{\prime}/l^{\prime}-k+1\}\{k/l\}
.

Proof.

Induction on
t
. The third equation is actually a consequence of the first.
∎

The equations cover all possible cases of composing MoTs. Notice that
in the third equation,
l^{\prime}-k+1>l
and thus can be used to turn an iterative
application of MoTs
ascending in
l
to one strictly descending in
l
.

2.3.
Term Substitution

Just like in typical type theories, we also need an (ordinary) term substitution operation which
replaces a variable with a term:

Definition 2.4.

The term substitution is defined as

\displaystyle x[u/y]

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}u&\text{if $x=y$}\\
x&\text{if $x\neq y$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\texttt{box}\ t[u/y]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{box}\ (t[u/y])

\displaystyle\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t[u/y]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ (t[u/y])

(Notice implicit
\alpha
renaming here)

\displaystyle\lambda x.t[u/y]

\displaystyle:=\lambda x.(t[u/y])

\displaystyle s\ t[u/y]

\displaystyle:=(s[u/y])\ (t[u/y])

The following lemma is standard.

Lemma 2.5.

If
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma,x:S,\Gamma^{\prime};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash t:T
and

\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vdash s:S
,
then
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash t[s/x]:T
.

Proof.

Induction on
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma,x:S,\Gamma^{\prime};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash t:T
.
∎

MoTs and term substitutions commute depending on their numerical relation:

Lemma 2.6.

If
x
is in
n
’th context
5
5

5

One might find this statement is not precise
as we use an abstract name representation. Nonetheless, this is possible if one
uses more concrete name representation, e.g. de Bruijn indices, in which
term substitutions must be defined with an offset of a world in which the substitutions
happens. Therefore this
n
in fact is known from the syntactic level.
and
n\leq l
, then

t[u/x]\{k/l\}=t\{k/l\}[u\{k/l-n\}/x]
.

Lemma 2.7.

If
x
is in
n
’th context and
n>l
, then
t[u/x]\{k/l\}=t\{k/l\}[u/x]
.

2.4.
Term Equivalence

Given a context and a type, term equivalence forms a PER, which equips with symmetry
and transitivity. Here we employ the
\beta\eta
equivalence between terms.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash s\approx t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx s:T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash s\approx t:T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx u:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash s\approx u:T}

Congruence rules:

\par\frac{x:T\in\Gamma}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vdash x\approx x:T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\vdash t\approx t^{\prime}:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{box}\ t\approx\texttt{box}\ t^{\prime}:\square T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx t^{\prime}:\square T\quad|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|=n}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t\approx\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t^{\prime}:T^{\prime}}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:S)\vdash t\approx t^{\prime}:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t\approx\lambda x.t^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx t^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash s\approx s^{\prime}:S}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\ s\approx t^{\prime}\ s^{\prime}:T}

\beta
equivalence:

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\vdash t:T\quad|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|=n}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ (\texttt{box}\ t)\approx t\{n/0\}:T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:S)\vdash t:T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vdash s:S}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vdash(\lambda x.t)s\approx t[s/x]:T}

\eta
equivalence:

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:\square T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx\texttt{box}\ \texttt{unbox}_{1}\ t:\square T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:S\longrightarrow T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx\lambda x.(t\ x):S\longrightarrow T}

Notice that a MoT is used in the
\beta
equivalence for
\square T
.

Lemma 2.8 (Presupposition).

If
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx t^{\prime}:T
, then
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:T
and
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t^{\prime}:T
.

Proof.

Induction on
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx t^{\prime}:T
.
∎

3.
Combining Modal Transformation and Substitution

In the previous section, we showed that Kripke-style systems have two seemingly unrelated
operations: MoTs and term substitutions. This unfortunately poses
difficulties in representing simultaneous substitutions between context stacks. In typical type
systems, simultaneous substitutions are morphisms between contexts and conceptually just a list of
terms. With context stacks, a list of terms would not be sufficient. Consider how to
represent a simultaneous substitution
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}
. Intuition says that a list of
terms in
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
can be used to substitute
\Delta^{\prime}
, but how do we handle
\Delta

and in which stack terms substituting
\Delta

should be well-typed? In this section, we give
unified
substitutions, which unify
MoTs and term substitutions as a single representation. Moreover, unified
substitutions have identity and are closed under composition. Therefore we can
organize a category with context stacks as objects and unified substitutions as
morphisms, which is very helpful in later developments of normalization. We also show how unified
substitutions are applied to terms.

To obtain enough intuition and arrive at our final solution, let us first consider a
subquestion: can we view MoTs as substitutions?

3.1.
MoTs as Substitutions

First of all, MoTs are an operation transforming a term in
one context stack to another. In general, when viewed as a morphism, a MoT has the following
type according to
Lemma

2.2
:

\displaystyle\{k/l\}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{0};\cdots;(\Gamma_{k},\Delta_{0});\cdots;\Delta_{l}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0};\cdots;\Delta_{l}

which is orthogonal to term substitutions defined by
substituting variables with terms. By
Lemma

2.3
, we require MoTs to
satisfy the following laws:

(if
l^{\prime}<l
)

\displaystyle\{k/l\}\circ\{k^{\prime}/l^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle=\{k^{\prime}/l^{\prime}\}\circ\{k/l+k^{\prime}-1\}

(if
l\leq l^{\prime}<l+k
)

\displaystyle\{k/l\}\circ\{k^{\prime}/l^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle=\{k+k^{\prime}-1/l\}

If we are to represent MoTs as morphisms, then this representation
must be general enough to represent the compositional closure of MoTs.
Hence our next question is what is this compositional closure?

3.2.
An Intuition

First, let us compare MoTs with term substitutions: consider two adjacent term
substitutions
t[u/x][u^{\prime}/y]
where
x
and
y
are different (otherwise the second
substitution is voided). Then we can merge these two term substitutions into one by
simultaneously substituting both
x
and
y
for
u
and
u^{\prime}
, respectively. Can we do
the same for MoTs? If that is possible, then effectively we can merge arbitrary number
of adjacent MoTs into one. Let us first consider some examples.

3.2.1.
An Example for Modal Weakening

From
Lemma

2.2
, we know that
\{1+k/l\}
denotes the
morphism
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{1};\cdots;(\Gamma_{k},\Delta_{0});\cdots;\Delta_{l}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Delta_{0};\cdots;\Delta_{l}
. Now consider more concretely,

\displaystyle\{2/3\}:

\displaystyle\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1};(\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{0});\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Rightarrow\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0};\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}

\displaystyle\{2/1\}:

\displaystyle\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1};(\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{0});\Delta_{1};\Gamma_{1}^{\prime};(\Gamma_{2}^{\prime},\Delta_{2});\Delta_{3}\Rightarrow\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1};(\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{0});\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}

Where
\epsilon
is the empty stack.
Therefore, we derive

\displaystyle\{2/3\}\circ\{2/1\}:\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1};(\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{0});\Delta_{1};\Gamma_{1}^{\prime};(\Gamma_{2}^{\prime},\Delta_{2});\Delta_{3}\Rightarrow\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0};\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}

We obtain from
Lemma

2.3
that two modal weakenings can be commuted:

\displaystyle\{2/3\}\circ\{2/1\}=\{2/1\}\circ\{2/4\}

Notice that effectively these two modal weakenings are somewhat independent: they
modal-weaken
\Delta_{0}
and
\Delta_{2}
, respectively. Thus, if we begin
with the original context stack
\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0};\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}
,
then we can turn
\{2/3\}\circ\{2/1\}
into a single
simultaneous
modal
weakening operation:
\{(2/3);(2/1)\}
. In general, any composition of modal
weakenings can be represented by
\{\overrightarrow{(k_{i}/l_{i})}\}
, where
l_{i}
are in strictly
descending order for the sake of obtaining a canonical representation. Thus we can conclude

\displaystyle\{(2/3);(2/1)\}=\{2/3\}\circ\{2/1\}=\{2/1\}\circ\{2/4\}

or equivalently in commutative diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1};(\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{0});\Delta_{1};\Gamma_{1}^{\prime};(\Gamma_{2}^{\prime},\Delta_{2});\Delta_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0};\Delta_{1};\Gamma_{1}^{\prime};(\Gamma_{2}^{\prime},\Delta_{2});\Delta_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1};(\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{0});\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0};\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{2/1\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{2/4\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(2/3);(2/1)\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{2/1\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{2/3\}}

We observe that such simultaneous modal weakening satisfies the following inductive
equations if all
k
’s are at least
1
:

\displaystyle\{\}

\displaystyle=\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}

\displaystyle\{(k/l);\overrightarrow{(k_{i}/l_{i})}\}

\displaystyle=\{k/l\}\circ\{\overrightarrow{(k_{i}/l_{i})}\}

Note that on the left hand side, in order to write the
k
’s and
l
’s in the
simultaneous form, we have already assumed
l
’s are in descending order.
These equations allow us to compose two simultaneous modal weakenings by
unpacking them into individual modal weakenings and use
Lemma

2.3
to adjust the
order so that all
l
’s are in descending order. The lemma ensures that a strict
descending order is always possible even some
k
’s are
0
. That allows us to
conclude that modal weakening is closed under composition.

3.2.2.
Examples with Fusion

The previous observation works very well in systems without the axiom
T
6
6

6

Or
equivalently systems where
unbox
levels are not
0
or modal fusion is not
permitted
. Once modal fusion is involved, complication arrives. Consider the
following example:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon;\Gamma_{0},\Delta,\Gamma_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};(\Delta,\Gamma_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0/0\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0/0\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1/0\}}

In this example,
\{0/0\}
in the diagonal not only fuse
\Gamma_{0}
and
\Gamma_{1}
,
but also introduces an extra (unspecified)
\Delta
. This is because modal fusion
necessarily carries a notion of local weakening. Due to our representation of names, details
involving the length of
\Delta
is not exposed syntactically in MoTs,
but in other representations, e.g. de Bruijn indices, we will have to handle this
problem explicitly. To capture this complication, we need to allow modal fusion to
perform arbitrary local weakenings.

Even if local weakening caused by modal fusion is not involved, fusion breaks other
assumptions that were safe when only modal weakening are present. For instance,
previously, we stated that individual model weakenings from one simultaneous modal
weakening can be performed in isolation. Here is a counterexample with the presence
of modal fusion:

\displaystyle\{0/2\}:\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1};(\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1});\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Rightarrow\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1};(\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{0});\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}

Notice that
(\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{0})
and
\Delta_{1}
are fused. Thus we have

\displaystyle\{2/3\}\circ\{0/2\}:\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1};(\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1});\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Rightarrow\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0};\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}

where
\{2/3\}
comes from the previous example.

Even though the numbers in the
l
position are already in descending order, the
effects of
\{2/3\}
and
\{0/2\}
apply to two fused contexts, namely
\Delta_{0}
and

\Delta_{1}
. This example seems to suggest a complex operation which first does local weakening, then some fusions and
at last modal weakening.

Definition 3.1.

The
not-so-good
MoT is defined as follows:

\displaystyle t\{\overrightarrow{m},n,k/l\}:=t\underbrace{[\uparrow_{m}]}_{m\in\overrightarrow{m}}\{0/l\}^{n}\{k+1/l\}

where
\uparrow_{m}
is the weakening morphism which weakens the
m
’th context from
top of the stack:
\uparrow_{m}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma_{m},\Delta);\cdots;\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{m};\cdots;\Gamma_{0}
.

Clearly, this operation is way too complex for a valuable investigation.
Nevertheless, we obtain one useful observation: if modal fusion embeds local
weakenings, a special case of term substitutions, why not add
more complex term substitutions to MoTs?

3.3.
Unified Substitution

To best motivate our unified representation of MoTs and term
substitutions, let us take a closer look at an example of multiple applications of
MoTs:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{\Gamma}=\epsilon;}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{0};}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{0};}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{1};}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3});}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{2};}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{\Delta}=\epsilon;}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{0};}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2};\Gamma_{3};}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{4}}

This diagram represents a composition of a number of MoTs and
contains both modal fusion and modal weakening. For example, we can see that

\Gamma_{4}
is modal-weakened with
\Delta_{2}
prepended. Modal fusion happens
consecutively for
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma_{3}
. These three separate contexts
in the stack are fused into one, and then the resulting context is modal-weakened. The
arrows in the diagram denotes the correspondence of contexts before and after the
simultaneous MoT. Obviously, all these arrows are just some local
weakening typed in the context stacks to the left of the ends of arrows. Moreover, if
we replace these arrows with more complex local substitutions, then all
\Gamma_{i}
at the
bottom can be some arbitrary
\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}
. Additional bookkeeping is necessary to
maintain the relative positions for the contexts in domain and codomain context
stacks. Achieving all these requirements, we introduce unified substitution defined as follows:

Definition 3.2.

A unified substitution
\overrightarrow{\sigma}
, or just
substitution
, between context stacks is defined as

(Local substitutions,
Subst
)

\displaystyle\sigma,\delta

\displaystyle:=()\;|\;\sigma,t/x

(Unified substitutions,
Substs
)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\sigma},\overrightarrow{\delta}

\displaystyle:=\varepsilon;\sigma\;|\;\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{\sigma}

\frac{\sigma:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow\Gamma}{{\varepsilon;\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow\epsilon;\Gamma}\quad\frac{\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Delta}\quad|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}|=n\quad\sigma:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta}{\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Delta};\Delta}

where local substitution
\sigma:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow\Gamma
is defined as a list of terms as
usual. Moreover, the
offset

n
is subject to the
unbox
level constraint of the
system. That is, in system
K
,
n
must be
1
; in system
T
,
n
can be
0
or

1
, etc.

The core idea of this definition is simple: a local substitution
\sigma

replaces variables in a context
\Gamma
and an offset
n
specifies the
correspondence of contexts. Just as context stacks must be non-empty and consist of
at least one context, a unified substitution must have a topmost local substitution.
By definition, we know that a unified substitution always has a topmost
local substitution. To illustrate, the morphism in the previous diagram can be represented as

\varepsilon;\textsf{id};\Uparrow^{3}\!\!{\textsf{wk}}_{1};\Uparrow^{0}\!\!{\textsf{wk}}_{2};\Uparrow^{0}\!\!{\textsf{wk}}_{3};\Uparrow^{2}\!\!{\textsf{id}}
where
id
is local
identities and

\textsf{wk}_{i}:\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0};\Delta_{1};(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3})\Rightarrow{\Gamma_{i}}

are appropriate local weakenings. We break down this representation:

(1)

We start with
\varepsilon;\textsf{id}:\epsilon;\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\epsilon;\Gamma_{0}
.

(2)

We add an offset
3
and a local weakening
\textsf{wk}_{1}
, forming

\varepsilon;\textsf{id};\Uparrow^{3}\!\!{\textsf{wk}}_{1}:\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0};\Delta_{1};(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3})\Rightarrow\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1}
. The offset
3
adds three contexts to
the domain stack (
\Delta_{0}
,
\Delta_{1}
and
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3}
). Local weakening

\textsf{wk}_{1}
extracts
\Gamma_{1}
from
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3}
.

(3)

We extend the substitution to

\varepsilon;\textsf{id};\Uparrow^{3}\!\!{\textsf{wk}}_{1};\Uparrow^{0}\!\!{\textsf{wk}}_{2}:\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Delta_{0};\Delta_{1};(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3})\Rightarrow\epsilon;\Gamma_{0};\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
. Since the offset associated
with
\textsf{wk}_{2}
is
0
, no context is added to the domain stack. This effectively represents fusion.
\textsf{wk}_{2}
is
similar to
\textsf{wk}_{1}
.

(4)

The rest proceeds very similarly.

Clearly, if we change the local substitutions in the example above to contain more
terms, we obtain unified substitutions that also do nontrivial term substitutions.
Subsequently, we may simply write

\overrightarrow{\sigma};\sigma
instead of
\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{\sigma}
. In particular,
we will write
\overrightarrow{\sigma};\textsf{wk}
instead of
\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{\textsf{wk}}
and

\overrightarrow{\sigma};\textsf{id}
instead of
\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{\textsf{id}}
. We often omit offsets
that are
1
for readability.

Next, we shall define some operations on unified substitutions.
In order to define
composition, we describe two essential operations:
truncation

(
\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n}
) drops
n
substitutions from a unified substitution
\overrightarrow{\sigma}
;

truncation offset
(
\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})
) computes when given a unified substitution
\overrightarrow{\sigma}
the
total
number of contexts that need to be dropped from the domain context stack, given that we truncate
\overrightarrow{\sigma}
by
n
.

The
\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})
operation takes as input the unified substitution

\overrightarrow{\sigma}
and
n
, the number of local substitutions to be
dropped from
\overrightarrow{\sigma}
. It computes the sum of
n
leading
offsets, called
truncation offset
. Let

\overrightarrow{\sigma}=\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime};\Uparrow^{k_{n}}\!\!{\sigma_{n}};\ldots;\Uparrow^{k_{1}}\!\!{\sigma_{1}}
, then
\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})=k_{n}+\ldots+k_{1}
and
\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n}=\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}
. For the operation to be meaningful,
n
must be less than
|\Delta|
.

(Truncation Offset)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\_},{\_})

\displaystyle:(\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Delta})\to\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{0})

\displaystyle:=0

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{\sigma}},{1+m})

\displaystyle:=n+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{m})

Truncation simply drops
n
local
substitutions regardless of the offset that is associated with
each local substitution.

(Truncation)

\displaystyle\_\mid{\_}

\displaystyle:(\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Delta})\to(n:\mathbb{N})\;\to\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Delta}\mid{n}

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{0}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\sigma}

\displaystyle(\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{m}\!\!{\sigma})\mid{1+n}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n}

Similar to truncation of substitutions, we rely on truncation of contexts, written as
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\mid{n}
which simply drops
n
contexts from the context stack
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
, i.e. if
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}=\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\Gamma_{1};\ldots;\Gamma_{n}
, then
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\mid{n}=\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}
.
Note that
n
must satisfy
n<|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}|
, otherwise the operation
would not be meaningful.
We emphasize that no further restrictions are placed on
n
and hence
our definitions apply to any of the combinations of Axioms
K
,
T
and
4

described in the introduction.

To organize the category of context stacks, we need to define the identity morphism.
The identity morphism is also a unified substitution:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle:=\varepsilon;\textsf{id};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{\textsf{id}};\cdots;\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{\textsf{id}}

We omit the subscript
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
when it can be inferred from the context and the
offset
1
:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}=\varepsilon;\textsf{id};\cdots;\textsf{id}

which matches our intuition even better.

Next, we will see that MoTs and term substitutions in
the form of
Section

2
are just special cases of unified substitutions.

3.3.1.
MoTs

As suggested in the previous section, we are readily able to represent MoTs
in the unified form. For clarity, let us distinguish the
cases for modal weakening and modal fusion. When it is modal weakening, we know
MoTs must be of the form
\{k+1/l\}
. Let
|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|=l
. In this case,

\displaystyle\{k+1/l\}

\displaystyle:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{1};\cdots;(\Gamma_{k+1},\Delta_{0});\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Delta_{0};\overrightarrow{\Delta}

\displaystyle\{k+1/l\}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon;\underbrace{\textsf{id};\cdots;\textsf{id}}_{|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}|};\Uparrow^{k+1}\textsf{wk};\underbrace{\textsf{id};\cdots;\textsf{id}}_{|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|}

where the offset
k+1
on the right adds
\Gamma_{1};\cdots;(\Gamma_{k+1},\Delta_{0})
to
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
in the domain
stack and
\textsf{wk}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{1};\cdots;(\Gamma_{k+1},\Delta_{0})\Rightarrow{\Delta_{0}}
.

Fusion is also easily defined:

\displaystyle\{0/l\}

\displaystyle:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2});\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2};\overrightarrow{\Delta}

\displaystyle\{0/l\}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon;\underbrace{\textsf{id};\cdots;\textsf{id}}_{|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}|};\textsf{wk}_{1};\Uparrow^{0}\!\textsf{wk}_{2};\underbrace{\textsf{id};\cdots;\textsf{id}}_{|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|}

where the offset
0
associated with
\textsf{wk}_{2}
allows us to fuse
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
, and

\textsf{wk}_{i}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\Rightarrow{\Gamma_{i}}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

3.3.2.
Term Substitution

Given
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\vdash u:U
, and

\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:U,\Gamma^{\prime});\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash t:T
, the substituted
term
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime});\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash t[u/x]:T
. Thus we can
give the following definition

\displaystyle[u/x]

\displaystyle:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime});\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:U,\Gamma^{\prime});\overrightarrow{\Delta}

\displaystyle[u/x]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon;\underbrace{\textsf{id};\cdots;\textsf{id}}_{|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}|};\sigma;\underbrace{\textsf{id};\cdots;\textsf{id}}_{|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|}

where
\sigma:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\Rightarrow{(\Gamma,x:U,\Gamma^{\prime})}
is
defined by replacing
x
with
u
and keep all other variables as is.

3.4.
Applying Unified Substitution

Now we have already defined unified substitution, but we have not answered how it can
be applied to terms. The definition is actually very straightforward.

Definition 3.3.

The simultaneous application of a unified substitution
\overrightarrow{\sigma}
is defined as

(lookup
x
in
\sigma
)

\displaystyle x[\overrightarrow{\alpha};\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\sigma(x)

\displaystyle(\texttt{box}\ t)[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{box}\ t[\overrightarrow{\sigma};()]

(note
m:=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})
)

\displaystyle(\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t)[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{unbox}_{m}\ (t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n}])

\displaystyle(\lambda x.t)[\varepsilon;\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\lambda x.t[\varepsilon;(\sigma,x/x)]

\displaystyle(\lambda x.t)[\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{\sigma}]

\displaystyle:=\lambda x.t[\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{(\sigma,x/x)}]

\displaystyle(s\ t)[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]

\displaystyle:=s[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]\ u[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]

There are a few comments worth making:

(1)

When we write
\overrightarrow{\sigma};()
, we should have written
\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{(})
but since the
offset is
1
, we take our liberty to omit it.

(2)

Notice that with unified substitution, we no longer need to do case analysis on

unbox
level as opposed to
Section

2.2
, because the result level can be
computed from the offsets in the unified substitution using the truncation offset operation.

(3)

Moreover, truncation offset respects the UL constraint imposed by the target
system. We can quickly verify this:

•

In system
K
, since
n
must be
1
,
\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{1})=1
must hold because

\overrightarrow{\sigma}
also only contains
1
’s.

•

In system
T
, the sum of zero or one
0
or
1
gives
0
or
1
.

•

In system
S4
, clearly the sum of any number of natural numbers remains
a natural number.

•

In system
K4
, the sum of a positive number of positive numbers is positive.

Thus we are sure
L
always gives valid UL for all valid inputs.

(4)

In the
\lambda
case, we extend the topmost substitution by substituting
x

for
x
. This extension resembles the usual
simultaneous substitution in typical type theories.

We can prove that the simultaneous application is well-defined:

Lemma 3.4.

If
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:T
and
\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
, then

\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]:T
.

Proof.

Induction on
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:T
. In the
unbox
case, recall that
\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\mid{n}
.
∎

3.5.
Composing Unified Substitution

Next we shall define composition of unified substitutions, and show that it satisfies the
categorical laws:

Definition 3.5.

The composition of unified substitutions is defined as

\displaystyle\_\;\circ\_\;

\displaystyle:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\to\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\to\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle(\varepsilon;\sigma)\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}

\displaystyle:=\varepsilon;(\sigma[\overrightarrow{\delta}])

\displaystyle(\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{\sigma})\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}

\displaystyle:=(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ{(\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{n})});\Uparrow^{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\delta}},{n})}\!\!{(\sigma[\overrightarrow{\delta}])}

where
\sigma_{1}[\overrightarrow{\sigma}_{2}]
is the iterative application of
\overrightarrow{\sigma}_{2}
to terms in

\sigma_{1}
as defined in the previous section.

The recursive case can be visualized in the following figure:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}_{2}},{n})};}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underbrace{\overrightarrow{\Delta}_{1}}_{m_{1}}}
;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underbrace{\overrightarrow{\Delta}_{n}}_{m_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\mid{n};}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}}
;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\mid{1};}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\overrightarrow{\sigma}_{1}
\overrightarrow{\sigma}_{2}\mid{n}
\sigma_{1}

where
\sum m_{i}=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}_{2}},{n})
.

With the definition of composition, we can prove the categorical laws for
substitutions. The laws are derived from the following lemma:

Lemma 3.6.

t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}]=t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}][\overrightarrow{\delta}]

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
t
. Most cases are immediate by IH. We only consdier the
case where
t=\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t^{\prime}[\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}]

\displaystyle=\texttt{unbox}_{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}},{n})}\ (t^{\prime}[(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta})\mid{n}])

(by
Lemmas

3.9
and
3.11
)

\displaystyle=\texttt{unbox}_{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\delta}},{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})})}\ (t^{\prime}[{(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n})}\circ(\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})})])

\displaystyle=\texttt{unbox}_{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})}\ (t^{\prime}[\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n}])[\overrightarrow{\delta}]

\displaystyle=\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ (t^{\prime})[\overrightarrow{\sigma}][\overrightarrow{\delta}]

∎

Lemma 3.7.

The following equations hold:

•

t[\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}][\overrightarrow{\sigma}]=t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]

•

t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}][\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}]=t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]

•

t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}_{1}][\overrightarrow{\sigma}_{2}\circ\overrightarrow{\sigma}_{3}]=t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}_{1}\circ\overrightarrow{\sigma}_{2}][\overrightarrow{\sigma}_{3}]

Proof.

Immediate from the previous lemma.
∎

Thus we have established that context stacks and unified substitutions constitute a
categorical structure.

3.6.
Other Properties of Unified Substitutions

In this section, we study some other basic properties of operations of unified
substitutions that will be very useful in later development. They are necessary
properties of truncation and truncation offset, and characterize the operations from an extensional
point of view. Let
\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
and
\overrightarrow{\delta}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}
:

Lemma 3.8 (Distributivity of Addition over Truncation Offset).

If
n+m<|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}|
, then

\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n+m})=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n}},{m})
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
.

Case

n=0
, then both sides are
\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{m})
.

Case

n=1+n^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{\sigma}},{1+n^{\prime}+m})

\displaystyle=k+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n^{\prime}+m})

(by IH)

\displaystyle=k+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n^{\prime}})+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n^{\prime}}},{m})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{\sigma}},{1+n^{\prime}})+\mathcal{O}({(\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{\sigma})\mid{(1+n^{\prime})}},{m})

∎

Lemma 3.9 (Distributivity of Composition over Truncation Offset).

If
n<|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}|
, then
\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}},{n})=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\delta}},{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})})
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
.

Case

n=0
, it is immediate.

Case

n=1+n^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({(\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{\sigma})\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}},{1+n^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}({(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{k});\Uparrow^{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\delta}},{k})}\!\!{(\sigma[\overrightarrow{\delta}])}},{1+n^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\delta}},{k})+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{k}},{n^{\prime}})

(by IH)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\delta}},{k})+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{k}},{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n^{\prime}})})

(by
Lemma

3.8
)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\delta}},{k+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n^{\prime}})})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\delta}},{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{\sigma}},{1+n^{\prime}})})

∎

Lemma 3.10 (Distributivity of Addition over Truncation).

If
n+m<|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}|
, then

\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{(n+m)}=(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n})\mid{m}
.

Proof.

Immediate.
∎

Lemma 3.11 (Distributivity of Composition over Truncation).

If
n<|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}|
,
then
(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta})\mid{n}=(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n})\circ(\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})})
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
.

Case

n=0
, immediate.

Case

n=1+n^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle((\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{\sigma})\circ\overrightarrow{\delta})\mid{(1+n^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=((\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{k});\Uparrow^{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\delta}},{k})}\!\!{(\sigma[\overrightarrow{\delta}])}\mid{(1+n^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{k})\mid{n^{\prime}}

(by IH)

\displaystyle=\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n^{\prime}}\circ((\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{k})\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n^{\prime}})})

\displaystyle=\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n^{\prime}}\circ(\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{(k+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n^{\prime}}))})

\displaystyle=(\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{\sigma})\mid{(1+n^{\prime})}\circ(\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{\sigma}},{1+n^{\prime}})})

∎

3.7.
Comments on Unified Substitution

It is worth noting that unified substitutions are quite adaptive. Recall that we can
control the range of
unbox
levels to tune which modal logic the system corresponds
to. As long as the
unbox
level constraint is maintained for offsets
in all unified substitutions, then unified substitutions can be used in all four
systems we have been discussing as a representation for simultaneous substitutions.
In fact, the observation of unified substitutions is far reaching, and can be used to
simplify existing work, as to be shown in the next section.

4.
A Simpler Idempotent Modal Type Theory

Gratzer
et al. 2019
;
Clouston 2018
discussed Fitch-style
idempotent S4 modal type theories and provided their categorical semantics, substitution
calculi, as well as a normalization algorithm. It appears that many existing
applications of comonadic modal type theories are idempotent S4, so a simpler
formulation definitely helps. In this section, we briefly discuss
their calculi and define an equivalent formulation with a simpler substitution
calculus.

4.1.
Idempotency in Fitch Style

This type theory is given by
Clouston 2018
and generalized with
dependent types by
Gratzer
et al. 2019
. We will only discuss the simply
typed version. The result can be carried over to the dependently typed case in

Gratzer
et al. 2019
as the
fundamental idea remains the same. In this section, we only discuss the core idea.

In idempotent
S4
, the critical rules are:

\frac{x:T\in\Gamma^{\prime}\quad\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}\notin\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}},\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash x:T}\par\frac{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}\vdash t:T}{\Gamma\vdash\texttt{box}\ t:\square T}\par\frac{\Gamma^{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}\vdash t:\square T}{\Gamma\vdash\texttt{unbox}\ t:T}

In the variable rule, according to Kripke semantics,
x
is looked up in the current
“world”, as ensured by
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}\notin\Gamma^{\prime}
. The introduction rule for
\square

again simply accesses the next world by inserting a

in the context. The
elimination rule is very different as it does not have an
unbox
level. This is
because with idempotency, all previously accessed worlds can no longer be
distinguished from each other. In other words, we cannot tell whether a world is accessed one world
ago, two worlds ago, or more. In the elimination rule, this is reflected by the operation

\Gamma^{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}
, which removes all

’s from
\Gamma
. After removal of

’s, all previous worlds are considered as one and visible in the current
world.
Their relative positions are lost.

If we look closer, in the reading of Kripke semantics, the formulation already seems
redundant: despite the fact that we cannot distinguish more than two kinds of worlds
(all of the previous
v.s. the current), syntactically we still maintain more than one

in the
context. Moreover, the substitution calculus given by

Gratzer
et al. 2019
seems quite complex because it needs to handle
more than one

’s. For the simplest example, the identity substitution is
typed as

\frac{\Gamma\rhd_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}\Delta}{\Gamma\vdash\textsf{id}:\Delta}

where
\Gamma\rhd_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}\Delta
is morally a judgment saying
\Delta
is
\Gamma

with more

’s. But this judgment has the following rule

\frac{}{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}\rhd_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}

which makes sense but fails to line up with the immediate intuition. As a result, the eventual
substitution calculus is rather complex and does not have a clear
structure. Hence, in this section, we develop an equivalent system which takes
advantages of certain observations, including unified substitution, and has a simpler
formulation.

4.2.
Idempotency in Kripke Style

In this section, we develop our target system in Kripke style; there is no technical
reason, as we just want to be consistent with our philosophical choice. The system can
be easily replayed in Fitch style by using

instead of a context stack. Even
though we call it a
stack
, there are only two contexts in it, it is clearer to
write out the contexts explicitly:

\frac{x:T\in\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash x:T}\par\frac{(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime});\cdot\vdash t:T}{\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\texttt{box}\ t:\square T}\par\frac{\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\vdash t:\square T}{\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\texttt{unbox}\ t:T}

An experienced eye might spot the similarity between these two contexts and the
dual-context style. They definitely have undeniable relation but they come from
different inspirations. As explained in
Section

1
, dual-context formulation
comes from the categorization of validity and truth. In this system, the contexts are
still Kripke-style: for
\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}
,
\Gamma
denotes assumptions from all
previously accessed world while
\Gamma^{\prime}
captures the current one. This view explains
the introduction rule and the elimination rule. In the introduction rule, we just
insert the assumptions
\Gamma^{\prime}
in the current world in the conclusion to those in the previous
worlds in the premise, because we are entering a new world and differences between the current world
and previous ones are vanished. Similarly, when eliminating, all assumptions in
previous worlds become visible after being moved to the current world.

Next we do some basic syntactic sanity check. The following lemma is needed to justify the check:

Lemma 4.1.

If
(\Gamma,x:S);\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:T
, then
\Gamma;(x:S,\Gamma^{\prime})\vdash t:T
.

Proof.

Induction on
(\Gamma,x:S);\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:T
.
∎

We check the local soundness for
\square
:

\frac{\frac{(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime});\cdot\vdash t:T}{\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\vdash\texttt{box}\ t:\square T}}{\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\texttt{unbox}\ (\texttt{box}\ t):T}\par\Longrightarrow\par\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:T

We implicitly applied
Lemma

4.1
to get
\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:T
from
(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime});\cdot\vdash t:T
.

We also check the local completeness:

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:\square T\par\Longrightarrow\par\frac{\frac{\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\vdash t:\square T}{(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime});\cdot\vdash\texttt{unbox}\ t:T}}{\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\texttt{box}\ (\texttt{unbox}\ t):\square T}

Again, we applied
Lemma

4.1
to get

\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\vdash t:\square T
from

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:\square T
. Thus these rules syntactically make sense,
but we must make sure we are still talking about the same system.

Comparing two formulations, it is quite easy to see why these two systems ought to be
equivalent. After all, the latter system simply stops maintaining a difference which
the system cannot tell. Moreover, we do not need to define the
\_^{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}
operation
because we simply make
\Gamma
visible by moving it to the current context in the
elimination rule.

We can formally show their equivalence. It is quite easy after we understand that we only
need to know whether a context is the current one or not. We define a function which
finds the current world in the Fitch-style system:

\displaystyle S(\cdot)

\displaystyle:=(\cdot,\cdot)

(where
(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}):=S(\Gamma)
)

\displaystyle S(\Gamma,x:T)

\displaystyle:=(\Delta,(\Delta^{\prime},x:T))

\displaystyle S(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}})

\displaystyle:=(\Gamma^{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}},\cdot)

then we have

Lemma 4.2.

If
\Gamma\vdash t:T
in Fitch style and
(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})=S(\Gamma)
, then

\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash t:T
in Kripke style.

Proof.

By induction on the typing judgment.
∎

The other direction, to some extend, justifies the
\rhd_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}}
relation in the
previous section, because of the unnecessary maintenance of extra

’s in the
context. We first need a relation between contexts in both styles:

Definition 4.3.

\Gamma
(in Fitch style) is more complex than
\Delta
(in Kripke style), if

\Gamma
is just
\Delta
with any number of

’s inserted.

Note that this relation literally just adds

’s to
\Delta
. After all,

\Delta
is in Kripke style and therefore there is no

in it.

Lemma 4.4.

•

If
\cdot;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:T
in Kripke style, then
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:T
in
Fitch style.

•

If
\Delta;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:T
in Kripke style and
\Gamma
is more complex
than
\Delta
, then
\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}},\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:T
in Fitch style.

Proof.

By induction on the typing judgment.
∎

4.3.
Unified Substitution with Idempotency

Inspired by the idea of unified substitution in
Section

3.3
, we can also define
a version of idempotent S4. Unlike the “full blown” unified substitution, due to
idempotency, there is no longer need to keep track of
unbox
levels and thus the
structure of substitution is largely contracted:

Definition 4.5.

A unified substitution for idempotent S4,
\sigma:\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}
, is defined as two local substitutions:

(1)

\sigma_{1}:\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\Rightarrow\Delta
,

(2)

\sigma_{2}:\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
.

We let
\sigma:=\sigma_{1};\sigma_{2}
.

From the view of unified substitution,
\sigma_{2}:\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}

represents the top level substitution. However,
\sigma_{1}:\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\Rightarrow\Delta
is curious as it moves
\Gamma
to the current context in domain. This
treatment is correct and necessary as terms in
\Delta
must have access to all previous worlds.

In order to define the application of unified substitution to terms, given

\sigma:\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}
, we need to supply two morphisms:

(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime});\cdot\Rightarrow(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime});\cdot
and

\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\Rightarrow\cdot;(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})
for substituting
box
and

unbox
. We define

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}_{1}

\displaystyle:\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\Rightarrow(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})

(concateneating
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
)

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}_{2}

\displaystyle:(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime});\cdot\Rightarrow\cdot

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}_{2}

\displaystyle:=()

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}

\displaystyle:(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime});\cdot\Rightarrow(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime});\cdot

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=\widehat{\sigma}_{1};\widehat{\sigma}_{2}

Note that we rely on
Lemma

4.1
to type check
\widehat{\sigma}_{1}
. In
particular, since
\sigma_{2}:\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
, by

Lemma

4.1
, we can move
\Gamma
to the current context, and thus

\sigma_{2}:\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}

\displaystyle:\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\Rightarrow\cdot

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}

\displaystyle:=()

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}_{2}

\displaystyle:\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\Rightarrow(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}

\displaystyle:\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\Rightarrow\cdot;(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{\sigma}_{1};\widetilde{\sigma}_{2}

So we can define the application of unified substitution as

\displaystyle x[\sigma_{1};\sigma_{2}]

\displaystyle:=\sigma_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\texttt{box}\ t[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{box}\ (t[\widehat{\sigma}])

\displaystyle\texttt{unbox}\ t[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{unbox}\ (t[\widetilde{\sigma}])

Other cases are omitted.

Clearly, unified substitution has much clearer structure as it simply is a combination
of two local substitutions. We can define the identity substitution as follows:

id

\displaystyle:\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}

id

\displaystyle:=\textsf{wk}_{1};\textsf{wk}_{2}

where
\textsf{wk}_{1}:\cdot;(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\Rightarrow\Gamma
and
\textsf{wk}_{2}:\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}
are two evident local substitutions. This definition, again, is much cleaner
and easy to understand.

At last, we define composition:

\displaystyle\_\circ\_

\displaystyle:\Gamma^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Gamma^{\prime\prime};\Delta^{\prime\prime}\to\Gamma;\Delta\Rightarrow\Gamma^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\to\Gamma;\Delta\Rightarrow\Gamma^{\prime\prime};\Delta^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle(\sigma_{1};\sigma_{2})\circ\delta

\displaystyle:=\sigma_{1}[\widetilde{\delta}];\sigma_{2}[\delta]

Note that

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}

\displaystyle:\cdot;(\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})\Rightarrow\cdot;\Gamma^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta}

\displaystyle:\cdot;(\Gamma,\Delta)\Rightarrow\cdot;(\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})

and
\sigma_{2}[\delta]
denote iterative application of substitution as defined above,
so the definition is well-defined.

We can verify that under these definitions of identity and composition, we have a
category of contexts in Kripke style:

Lemma 4.6.

id
and
\_\circ\_
satisfy categorical laws.

Proof.

Follow the definition.
∎

Here we conclude the substitution calculus for idempotent S4. We leave the
generalization to dependent types, an explicit substitution calculus in the style of

Section

6
and a normalization algorithm as future work.

5.
Presheaf Model and Normalization by Evaluation

In this section, we build a presheaf model for the modal type theory
from which we extract a normalization by evaluation (NbE) algorithm.

5.1.
Unified Weakening

To understand the Kripke structure of context stacks, we restrict unified
substitutions to only variables. If the resulting sets of unified substitutions also
form a category, then we can use this category as the base category of a presheaf
category, from which we can compute normal forms of terms. Indeed this plan works
out. First let us see how this restricted version of unified substitutions, unified
weakening, is defined:

Definition 5.1.

A unified weakening
\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta}
is:

(Unified weakenings)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\gamma}:=\varepsilon\;|\;q(\overrightarrow{\gamma})\;|\;p(\overrightarrow{\gamma})\;|\;\overrightarrow{\gamma};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{}

\frac{}{\varepsilon:\epsilon;\cdot\Rightarrow_{w}\epsilon;\cdot}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta};\Delta}{q(\overrightarrow{\gamma}):\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:T)\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta};(\Delta,x:T)}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta};\Delta}{p(\overrightarrow{\gamma}):\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:T)\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta};\Delta}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta}\quad|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}|=n}{\overrightarrow{\gamma};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta};\cdot}

In the last rule, the offset
n
is again parametric and its choice
determines which modal logic the system corresponds to.

The
q
constructor is the identity extension of the weakening

\overrightarrow{\gamma}
, while
p
accommodates weakening of an individual
context. These constructors are typical in the category of
weakenings. To accommodate MoTs, we add to the category of
weakenings the last rule which modal-transforms a context stack.
Note that we also write
\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}
for the identity unified weakening.
Since a unified weakening is also a unified substitution after all, its
truncation, truncation offset, composition, application and their properties are all inherited from
unified substitutions. Intuitively, unified weakenings is unified substitutions in which
local substitutions are all local weakenings. This intuition suggests that unified
weakenings are closed under composition:

Lemma 5.2.

If
\overrightarrow{\gamma}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}
and
\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}
, then

\overrightarrow{\gamma}^{\prime}\circ\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

Induction on
\overrightarrow{\gamma}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}
and analyze
\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}
when necessary.
∎

With
\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}
and closure
of composition, context stacks and unified weakenings form a category, called
\mathpzc{W}
, (in fact a
subcategory of the one with unified substitutions as morphisms).
Moreover, unified weakenings are sufficient to encode any MoT.

5.2.
Presheaf Model

We consider the presheaf category over
\mathpzc{W}
, namely the category with objects as
functors
\mathpzc{W}^{op}\Rightarrow\mathpzc{Set}
and morphisms as natural transformations. We
interpret both types and contexts as presheaves (denoted as
F
and
G
), and terms as
natural transformations.

5.2.1.
Interpreting Types and Contexts

Given two presheaves
F
and
G
, we form a presheaf exponential
F{\hat{\longrightarrow}}G
by

\displaystyle F{\hat{\longrightarrow}}G

\displaystyle:\mathpzc{W}^{op}\Rightarrow\mathpzc{Set}

\displaystyle(F{\hat{\longrightarrow}}G)_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle:=\forall\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma}.F_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}\to G_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

Note that the right hand side is in fact a set of natural transformations.
This construction is justified by the Yoneda lemma.

To model
\square
, given a presheaf
F
, we define

\displaystyle{\hat{\square}}F

\displaystyle:\mathpzc{W}^{op}\Rightarrow\mathpzc{Set}

\displaystyle({\hat{\square}}F)_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle:=F_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot}

Alternatively,
{\hat{\square}}F=F\circ(-;\cdot)
.

We then move on to interpret types in the context stack calculus:

\displaystyle\llbracket\_\rrbracket

\displaystyle:\textsf{Typ}\to\mathpzc{W}^{op}\Rightarrow\mathpzc{Set}

\displaystyle\llbracket B\rrbracket

\displaystyle:=\textsf{Ne}\ B

\displaystyle\llbracket\square T\rrbracket

\displaystyle:={\hat{\square}}\llbracket T\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket S\longrightarrow T\rrbracket

\displaystyle:=\llbracket S\rrbracket{\hat{\longrightarrow}}\llbracket T\rrbracket

where
\textsf{Ne}\ T\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma}
is the set of well-formed neutral terms in
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
of type

T
as specified by
Section

2
. Similarly,
\textsf{Nf}\ T\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma}
denotes those
well-formed normal terms in
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
of type
T
.
\textsf{Ne}\ T
is a presheaf as all
neutrals can be applied to unified weakening and so is
\textsf{Nf}\ T
. We use
a
or
b
to
represent an element in
\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}
.

We interpret the contexts and context stacks iteratively:

\displaystyle\llbracket\_\rrbracket

\displaystyle:\textsf{Ctx}\to\mathpzc{W}^{op}\Rightarrow\mathpzc{Set}

\displaystyle\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket

\displaystyle:=\hat{\top}

\displaystyle\llbracket\Gamma,x:T\rrbracket

\displaystyle:=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\hat{\times}\llbracket T\rrbracket

where
\hat{\top}
and
\hat{\times}
denotes the pointwise constructions for a fixed terminal object
and products in presheaves, respectively. We use
*
to represent the only element of the singleton
set of our chosen terminal presheaf. We use
\rho
to represent a element in

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}
.

We interpret context stacks with
\Sigma
types as we need to know the number of
contexts to truncate from the parameter context stack:

\displaystyle\llbracket\_\rrbracket

\displaystyle:\overrightarrow{\textsf{Ctx}}\to\mathpzc{W}^{op}\Rightarrow\mathpzc{Set}

\displaystyle\llbracket\epsilon;\Gamma\rrbracket

\displaystyle:=\hat{\top}\hat{\times}\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

\displaystyle:=(\Sigma n<|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|.\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Delta}\mid{n}})\times\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

We use
\overrightarrow{\rho}
to represent a element in
\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
. By definition,

\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
must be a product type, in which the second projection
is the interpretation of the topmost context. We will use this fact very frequently in
our later development.

All these interpretations specify the object part. To prove the interpretations do
form functors, we also need to supply the morphism part. Namely given

\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
and a target presheaf
F
, we should supply a
function
F_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}\to F_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
. Given
a\in F_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}
, we write

a[\overrightarrow{\gamma}]:F_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
for the result of applying this function to
a
. We call
this function
monotonicity
. We
intentionally overload square brackets here because monotonicity is just the semantic
version of applying a unified weakening. We use the same notation for all interpretations and
they can be distinguished by the target to which monotonicity applies.

Lemma 5.3.

The interpretation of types
\llbracket T\rrbracket
is monotone.

Proof.

Given
\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
, the theorem requires to give a function

\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}\to\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
. We do induction on
T
.

Case

T=B
, then the goal becomes
\textsf{Ne}\ B\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma}\to\textsf{Ne}\ B\ \overrightarrow{\Delta}
, which is
just application of unified weakening on neutral terms.

Case

T=\square T^{\prime}
, then the goal becomes
\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot}\to\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Delta};\cdot}
. This function is given by

\displaystyle(a:\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot})\mapsto a[\overrightarrow{\gamma};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{}]

where
\overrightarrow{\gamma};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{}:\overrightarrow{\Delta};\cdot\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot
by definition of
unified weakening.

Case

T=S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}
, then the goal becomes
(\llbracket S\rrbracket{\hat{\longrightarrow}}\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket)_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}\to(\llbracket S\rrbracket{\hat{\longrightarrow}}\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket)_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
, or after more expansion:

\displaystyle(\forall\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma}.\llbracket S\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}\to\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}})\to(\forall\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta}.\llbracket S\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}\to\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}})

We give this function as

\displaystyle(f,\overrightarrow{\gamma}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta},a:\llbracket S\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}})\mapsto f(\overrightarrow{\gamma}\circ\overrightarrow{\gamma}^{\prime},a)

∎

Note that monotonicity of
\llbracket\square T\rrbracket
is ambiguous, because
\llbracket\square T\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}=\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot}
. For example, given
a:\llbracket\square T\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}
and
\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
, both
a[\overrightarrow{\gamma}]
and

a[\overrightarrow{\gamma};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{}]
are valid and equal. This ambiguity unfortunately carries through
the whole section but hopefully it does not create more problems than requiring a
small amount of extra attention.

Lemma 5.4.

The interpretation of context
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
is monotone.

Proof.

The monotonicity follows immediately from that of
\hat{\top}
, of
\hat{\times}
and of
\llbracket T\rrbracket
.
∎

Lemma 5.5.

The interpretation of context stacks
\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket
is monotone.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
. The first case is immediate. We only
consider the case where
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}=\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\Gamma
. Assuming

\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}
, we should define

\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\Gamma\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}\to\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\Gamma\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
. We
further expand the goal:

\displaystyle(\Sigma n<|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|.\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}\mid{n}})\times\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}\to(\Sigma n<|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|.\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Delta}\mid{n}})\times\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

It is immediate to give
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
from
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}
due to
the monotonicity of
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
. We then shall show

\Sigma n<|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|.\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Delta}\mid{n}}
from

\Sigma n<|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|.\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}\mid{n}}
.

(assumption)

\displaystyle n<|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|

(assumption)

\displaystyle\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}\mid{n}}

(truncation law for unified weakening)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\gamma}\mid{n}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\gamma}},{n})}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}\mid{n}\text{ and }\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\gamma}},{n})<|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|

(by IH)

\displaystyle\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Delta}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\gamma}},{n})}}

We let the first projection of the
\Sigma
in the conclusion to be
\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\gamma}},{n})
. To summarize, the proof
corresponds to the following function

\displaystyle((n,\overrightarrow{\rho}:\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}\mid{n}}),\rho:\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}})\mapsto((\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\gamma}},{n}),\overrightarrow{\rho}[\overrightarrow{\gamma}\mid{n}]),\rho[\overrightarrow{\gamma}])

This concludes the
monotonicity of
\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket
.
∎

5.2.2.
Functoriality of Interpretations

These monotonicity proofs constructively return elements in target
sets following given unified weakenings and can be extracted as programs. For rigorousness, we can also verify the
functorial laws so that we can confirm the interpretations are actually all
functors. In particular, given the object and morphism parts specified above, we only
need to show the identity and composition laws.

Lemma 5.6.

\llbracket T\rrbracket
is a functor.

Proof.

We first prove that given
a:\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}
,
a[\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}]
is the
identity function.

Case

T=B
, then it is the same as applying identity weakening to a neutral term,
so we surely get the same neutral term back.

Case

T=\square T^{\prime}
, then
a:\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot}
and we can immediately
resort to the IH.

Case

T=S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}
, then we should show

\displaystyle(f,\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma},a:\llbracket S\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}})\mapsto f(\overrightarrow{\gamma}\circ\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}},a)

By categorical laws of
\mathpzc{W}
, we know
\overrightarrow{\gamma}\circ\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}=\overrightarrow{\gamma}
. Thus
the definition just becomes an expansion of
f
.

We then show the composition law. Given
\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
and
