\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{}=\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot
)

\displaystyle\ \texttt{unbox}_{1}\ t\sim a\in\llparenthesis T^{\prime}\rrparenthesis_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot}

(by IH)

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{unbox}_{1}\ t\approx\downarrow^{T^{\prime}}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot}(a):T^{\prime}

(by congruence)

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{box}\ (\texttt{unbox}_{1}\ t)\approx\texttt{box}\ (\downarrow^{T^{\prime}}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot}(a)):\square T^{\prime}

(by
\eta
and transitivity)

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx\texttt{box}\ (\downarrow^{T^{\prime}}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot}(a)):T^{\prime}

Case

T=S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}
, let
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\Gamma:=\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
, then

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\ \forall\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma},s\sim b\in\llparenthesis S\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}.t[\overrightarrow{\gamma}]\ s\sim a(\overrightarrow{\gamma},b)\in\llparenthesis T^{\prime}\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

(by
Lemma

5.52
)

\displaystyle\ x\sim\uparrow^{S}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:S)}(x)\in\llbracket S\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:S)}

(by
H_{2}
and let
\overrightarrow{\gamma}
be
p(\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}):\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:S)\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\Gamma
)

\displaystyle\ t[p(\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}})]\ x\sim a(p(\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}),\uparrow^{S}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:S)}(x))\in\llparenthesis T^{\prime}\rrparenthesis_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:S)}

(by IH)

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:S)\vdash t\ x\approx\downarrow^{T^{\prime}}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:S)}(a(p(\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}),\uparrow^{S}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:S)}(x))):T^{\prime}

(by congruence)

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\lambda x.t\ x\approx\lambda x.\downarrow^{T^{\prime}}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:S)}(a(p(\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}),\uparrow^{S}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:S)}(x))):S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}

(by
\eta
and transitivity)

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx\lambda x.\downarrow^{T^{\prime}}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:S)}(a(p(\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}),\uparrow^{S}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:S)}(x))):S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}

∎

After setting up all the technical lemmas, we should formulate the semantic typing
judgment. But first, we will need to generalize the gluing model to contexts and
context stacks.

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\Gamma\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow\Gamma\times\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

\displaystyle:=\{((),*)\}

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\Gamma,x:T\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

\displaystyle:=\{((\sigma,t/x),(\rho,a))\;|\;\sigma\sim\rho\in\llparenthesis\Gamma\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}\text{ and }t\sim a\in\llparenthesis T\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}\}

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Rightarrow\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\times\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\epsilon;\Gamma\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

\displaystyle:=\{((\varepsilon;\sigma),(*,\rho))\;|\;\sigma\sim\rho\in\llparenthesis\Gamma\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}\}

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

\displaystyle:=\{((\overrightarrow{\sigma};n;\sigma),((n,\overrightarrow{\rho}),\rho))\;|\;\overrightarrow{\sigma}\sim\overrightarrow{\rho}\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis_{\overrightarrow{\Delta}\mid{n}}\text{ and }\sigma\sim\rho\in\llparenthesis\Gamma\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}\}

We need the follow lemmas to understand operations on
\overrightarrow{\sigma}\sim\overrightarrow{\rho}\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
.

Lemma 5.54.

If
\overrightarrow{\sigma}\sim\overrightarrow{\rho}\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
, then
\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})
.

Proof.

Immediate by induction on
n
.
∎

Lemma 5.55.

If
\overrightarrow{\sigma}\sim\overrightarrow{\rho}\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
, then
\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n}\sim\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n}\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\mid{n}\rrparenthesis_{\overrightarrow{\Delta}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})}}
.

Proof.

Immediate by induction on
n
.
∎

Lemma 5.56.

If
\overrightarrow{\sigma}\sim\overrightarrow{\rho}\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
, given
\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta}
, then

\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\gamma}\sim\overrightarrow{\rho}[\overrightarrow{\gamma}]\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Immediate by induction on
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
and apply
Lemma

5.50
when proper.
∎

Finally, we can define the judgment:

Definition 5.57.

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Vdash t:T:=\forall\overrightarrow{\sigma}\sim\overrightarrow{\rho}\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}.t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]\sim\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}(\overrightarrow{\rho})\in\llparenthesis T\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

5.7.2.
Semantic Typing Rules

Lemma 5.58.

\frac{x:T\in\Gamma}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\Vdash x:T}

Proof.

Immediate.
∎

Lemma 5.59.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\Vdash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Vdash\texttt{box}\ t:\square T}

Proof.

Assume
\overrightarrow{\sigma}\sim\overrightarrow{\rho}\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
and
\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}
, then

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\Vdash t:T

\displaystyle\ (\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|}\!\!{()})\sim((|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|,\overrightarrow{\rho}),*)\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\overrightarrow{\Delta};\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}

(by
H_{1}
)

\displaystyle\ t[\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|}\!\!{()}]\sim\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\overrightarrow{\Delta};\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}(((|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|,\overrightarrow{\rho}),*))\in\llparenthesis T\rrparenthesis_{\overrightarrow{\Delta};\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}

We also know

\displaystyle t[\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|}\!\!{()}]

\displaystyle=t[\overrightarrow{\sigma};()][\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}};\Uparrow^{|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|}\!\!{()}]

\displaystyle\approx\texttt{unbox}_{|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|}\ (\texttt{box}\ (t[\overrightarrow{\sigma};()]))

\displaystyle\approx\texttt{unbox}_{|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|}\ (\texttt{box}\ t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}])

Then

(by
Lemma

5.51
)

\displaystyle\ \texttt{unbox}_{|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|}\ (\texttt{box}\ t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}])\sim\llbracket\texttt{box}\ t\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}};\Uparrow^{|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|}\!\!{()}]\in\llparenthesis T\rrparenthesis_{\overrightarrow{\Delta};\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}

(by abstraction)

\displaystyle\ \texttt{box}\ t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]\sim\llbracket\texttt{box}\ t\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}(\overrightarrow{\rho})\in\llparenthesis\square T\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}

∎

Lemma 5.60.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Vdash t:\square T\quad|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|=n}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\Vdash\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t:T}

Proof.

Immediate by definition of
\llparenthesis\square T\rrparenthesis
and
Lemma

5.55
.
∎

Lemma 5.61.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma,x:S\Vdash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\Vdash\lambda x.t:S\longrightarrow T}

Proof.

Assume
\overrightarrow{\sigma}\sim\overrightarrow{\rho}\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}}
,
\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}
,

\overrightarrow{\gamma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}\Rightarrow_{w}\overrightarrow{\Delta}
and
s\sim b\in\llparenthesis S\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}
. Let

(\overrightarrow{\alpha};\sigma):=\overrightarrow{\sigma}
,
(\pi,\rho):=\overrightarrow{\rho}
and

(\overrightarrow{\beta};\gamma):=\overrightarrow{\gamma}
. Then we should prove that

\displaystyle(\lambda x.(t[\overrightarrow{\alpha}^{\prime};\sigma^{\prime},x/x]))\ s\sim\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}((\pi^{\prime},(\rho^{\prime},b)))\in\llparenthesis T\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}

where
(\overrightarrow{\alpha}^{\prime};\sigma^{\prime}):=\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\gamma}
and
(\pi^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}):=\overrightarrow{\rho}[\overrightarrow{\gamma}]
.

We need to construct
\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma,x:S\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}
from what we have. We
claim

\displaystyle(\overrightarrow{\alpha}^{\prime};\sigma^{\prime},s/x)\sim(\pi^{\prime},(\rho^{\prime},b))\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma,x:S\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}

This is because

\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\gamma}\sim\overrightarrow{\rho}[\overrightarrow{\gamma}]\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}
by
Lemma

5.56
. Therefore

\displaystyle t[\overrightarrow{\alpha}^{\prime};\sigma^{\prime},s/x]\sim\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}((\pi^{\prime},(\rho^{\prime},b)))\in\llparenthesis T\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}}

Comparing the goal and what we have, we just need to reason about the syntactic
term, and they are just one
\beta
rule away.
∎

Lemma 5.62.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Vdash t:S\longrightarrow T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Vdash s:S}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Vdash t\ s:T}

Proof.

Immediate by just applying the premises.
∎

5.7.3.
Fundamental Theorem

Since we have proven the semantic typing rules, we can conclude the fundamental
theorem:

Theorem 5.63.

(fundamental) If
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:T
, then
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\Vdash t:T
.

We also need to relate
\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}
and
\uparrow^{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}
:

Lemma 5.64.

\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}\sim\uparrow^{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}\in\llparenthesis\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}
.

Proof.

Immediate by induction on
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}
and apply
Lemma

5.52
.
∎

Theorem 5.65.

(soundness) If
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:T
, then
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx\textsf{nbe}^{T}_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}(t):T
.

Proof.

Applying the fundamental theorem, we have
t[\overrightarrow{\textsf{id}}]\sim\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}(\uparrow^{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}})\in\llparenthesis T\rrparenthesis_{\;\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}
. We conclude the goal
by applying
Lemmas

5.51
and
5.53
.
∎

6.
Normalization by Evaluation in Untyped Domain

In the previous section, we developed an NbE algorithm based on a presheaf
model. However, there are some difficulties for extending the presheaf model to
dependent types because of the complexity of
bookkeeping.
Kaposi and
Altenkirch 2017
shows how one can extract an NbE
algorithm from a category with families (CwF) model, but the proof is conceivably more
complex than
Abel 2013
and their formulation uses inverse
abstractions as the elimination for dependent functions instead of applications as it is
typically done.
Therefore, we choose to follow
Abel 2013
, and our first goal is to develop an NbE algorithm based on an untyped
domain. To simplify certain proofs, we will first extend the calculus in

Section

2
with explicit substitution, and then interpret the extended calculus
to an untyped domain, from which we extract the NbE algorithm.

6.1.
Explicit Substitution Calculus

We formulate a version of the calculus in
Section

2
with explicit
substitution. In this section, we complete the details of this calculus.

(application of unified substitution)

\displaystyle s,t,u

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \cdots\;|\;t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]

(Unified substitution,
Substs
)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\sigma},\overrightarrow{\delta}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{I}\;|\;\overrightarrow{\sigma},t/x\;|\;\textsf{wk}_{x}\;|\;\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{}\;|\;\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}

In addition to the existing typing rules, we add

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash t:T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]:T}

The following judgments specify well-formed unified substitutions:

\frac{}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{I}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\Gamma\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma},t/x:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:T)}\par\frac{}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:T)\vdash\textsf{wk}_{x}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\quad|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}|=n}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{}:\overrightarrow{\Delta};\cdot}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\delta}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}

The truncation offset function is defined recursively on the syntax of
\overrightarrow{\sigma}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{0})

\displaystyle:=0

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{I}},{1+n})

\displaystyle:=1+n

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({(\overrightarrow{\sigma},t/x)},{1+n})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{1+n})

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\textsf{wk}_{x}},{1+n})

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{I}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{m}\!\!{}},{1+n})

\displaystyle:=m+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}},{1+n})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\delta}},{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{1+n})})

The following lemma states the
condition when truncation offset is well-defined:

Lemma 6.1.

If
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}
and
n<|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|
, then
\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})<|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}|
.

Proof.

Induction on
n
and
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}
.
∎

In particular, it ensures the recursive cases always hit the defined cases.

We can also define truncation in a similar manner:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{0}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\sigma}

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{I}\mid{1+n}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{I}

\displaystyle(\overrightarrow{\sigma},t/x)\mid{1+n}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{1+n}

\displaystyle\textsf{wk}_{x}\mid{1+n}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{I}

\displaystyle(\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{m}\!\!{})\mid{1+n}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n}

\displaystyle(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta})\mid{1+n}

\displaystyle:=(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{1+n})\circ(\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{1+n})})

We can prove a similar lemma that quantifies the well behavior of truncation:

Lemma 6.2.

If
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}
and
n<|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|
, then

\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\mid{n}
.

Proof.

Induction on
n
and
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}
.
∎

We reexamine the properties in
Section

3.6
and all of them hold.

The equational theory of the newly added components all enjoy congruence and PER laws,
which I will not write out here. I will focus on the more interesting rules.
Let us first consider various application of unified substitution to terms:

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t[\overrightarrow{I}]\approx t:T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\delta}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\vdash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}]\approx t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}][\overrightarrow{\delta}]:T}

Then we look specifically into variables:

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\Gamma\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash x[\overrightarrow{\sigma},t/x]\approx t:T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\Gamma\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:T^{\prime}\quad y:T\in\Gamma}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash y[\overrightarrow{\sigma},t/x]\approx y[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]:T}\par\frac{y:T\in\Gamma}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:T^{\prime})\vdash y[\textsf{wk}_{x}]\approx y:T}

The we consider applying unified substitutions other terms:

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\quad\overrightarrow{\Delta};\cdot\vdash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{box}\ t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]\approx\texttt{box}\ (t[\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{}]):\square T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash t:\square T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta};\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}\quad\quad|\overrightarrow{\Delta}^{\prime}|=n\quad|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}|=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]\approx\texttt{unbox}_{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})}\ (t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n}]):T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta};\Delta\quad\overrightarrow{\Delta};\Delta,x:S\vdash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]\approx\lambda x.(t[(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\textsf{wk}_{x}),x/x]):S\longrightarrow T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\quad\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash s:S\longrightarrow T\quad\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash t:S}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash s\ t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]\approx(s[\overrightarrow{\sigma}])\ (t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]):T}

The advantage of this explicit substitution is that the
\beta
equivalences have
fixed syntax:

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\vdash t:T\quad|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|=n}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vdash\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ (\texttt{box}\ t)\approx t[\overrightarrow{I};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{}]:T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:S)\vdash t:T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vdash s:S}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vdash(\lambda x.t)s\approx t[\overrightarrow{I},s/x]:T}

The
\eta
expansion is simpler:

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:\square T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx\texttt{box}\ (\texttt{unbox}_{1}\ t):\square T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t:S\longrightarrow T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash t\approx\lambda x.t[\textsf{wk}_{x}]\ x:S\longrightarrow T}

We make the local weakening explicit in the function case.

Next, we consider the interaction between different unified substitutions, or the
algebraic axiomization of unified substitutions. We first begin with categorical laws:

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{I}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{I}\circ\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime\prime}\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash(\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}\circ\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime})\circ\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}\circ(\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}\circ\overrightarrow{\sigma}):\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

We next consider composition:

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime};\Gamma\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash t:T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\delta}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma},t/x\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}\approx(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}),t[\overrightarrow{\delta}]/x:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime};(\Gamma,x:T)}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\vdash\overrightarrow{\delta}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\quad|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|=n}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\vdash(\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{})\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}\approx(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}\mid{n});\Uparrow^{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\delta}},{n})}\!\!{}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\cdot}

At last, we consider the extensionality principles which stipulates how syntax should
interact:

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash\textsf{wk}_{x}\circ(\overrightarrow{\sigma},t/x)\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:T)}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx(\textsf{wk}_{x}\circ\overrightarrow{\sigma}),(x[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]/x):\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:T)}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta};\cdot\quad|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|>0\quad\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{1})=n}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{1};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{}:\overrightarrow{\Delta};\cdot}

After defining the equivalence between unified substitutions, we shall examine truncation offset and
truncation respect the equivalence:

Lemma 6.3.

If
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}
and
n<|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|
, then
\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}},{n})
.

Lemma 6.4.

If
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}
and
n<|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|
, then

\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\sigma}},{n})}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mid{n}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}\mid{n}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\mid{n}
.

Proof.

Induction on
n
and
\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vdash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}
.
∎

6.2.
Untyped Domain

In this section, we define the untyped domain in which we will operate and define the
NbE algorithm:

(Domain variables,
\mathbb{N}
)

\displaystyle z

(Domain terms,
D
)

\displaystyle a,b

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \Lambda(\overrightarrow{\rho},x,t)\;|\;\texttt{box}(a)\;|\;\uparrow^{T}(c)

(Neutral domain terms,
D^{\textsf{Ne}}
)

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ z\;|\;c\ d\;|\;\texttt{unbox}(k,c)

(Normal domain terms,
D^{\textsf{Nf}}
)

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \downarrow^{T}(a)

Env

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \textsf{Var}\rightharpoonup D

Envs

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\times\textsf{Env}

(Local evaluation environment,
Env
)

\displaystyle\rho

((Global) evaluation environment,
Envs
)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\rho}

The idea is that we interpret the syntactic terms into the domain
D
and perform
normalization in
\mathcal{D}
. Once it is done, we convert the value from
D
back to
syntactic normal form using two readback functions. Type-directed
\eta
expansion is performed
during the readback process.

In
D
, we distinguish local evaluation environments
Env
, which are partial
functions mapping a syntactic variable to a domain value, and global evaluation
environments
Envs
, which are functions mapping an
unbox
level to an offset of
unbox
level
and an
Env
. The purpose of the offsets is very similar to those in unified
substitutions, which are used to keep track of
unbox
levels for terms. In
D
, we
take one step further by modelling infinite context stacks. Thus a stream of offsets
and local environment
are just a function returning tuples. We of course will not need an
infinite stack and the typing judgments will only make use of some finite
prefix. Nevertheless, it is convenient to avoid thinking about finiteness in an untyped setting.
Notice that function abstractions in
D
,
\Lambda
, are modeled by three parameters:
the evaluation environment it captures, the syntactic variable and its
syntactic function body. This trick is defunctionalization
(
Reynolds 1998
)
which uses data to
represent functions.

At last, it is worth mentioning that
\uparrow
and
\downarrow
become
syntax

in the untyped domain, as opposed to reflection and reification
functions
in
the presheaf model. The roles of reflection and reification are taken by the readback
functions to be defined very shortly.

Next we define some operations on
Env
and
Envs
. First, since
Env
is partial,
we have
\textsf{emp}:\textsf{Env}
which is just undefined everywhere.

We can define our first
Envs
using
emp
:

empty

\displaystyle:\textsf{Envs}

\displaystyle\textsf{empty}(\_)

\displaystyle:=(1,\textsf{emp})

It is the empty environment which is just undefined everywhere.
We can extend an
Envs
in various ways. We overload the function
ext
to do so. Which
concrete instance to apply depends on the arguments. First, we can insert an
Env
to
an
Envs
:

ext

\displaystyle:\textsf{Envs}\to\mathbb{N}\to\textsf{Env}\to\textsf{Envs}

\displaystyle\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho},n,\rho)(0)

\displaystyle:=(n,\rho)

\displaystyle\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho},n,\rho)(1+m)

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\rho}(m)

If we leave the environment out, we stipulate it to be
emp
.

ext

\displaystyle:\textsf{Envs}\to\mathbb{N}\to\textsf{Envs}

\displaystyle\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho},n)

\displaystyle:=\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho},n,\textsf{emp})

If we further leave the natural number unspecified, we stipulate to be
1
:

ext

\displaystyle:\textsf{Envs}\to\textsf{Envs}

\displaystyle\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho},1)

We should also be able to insert a
D
to the top of the stack and bind
x
to it:

ext

\displaystyle:\textsf{Envs}\to\textsf{Var}\to D\to\textsf{Envs}

(where
(k,\rho):=\overrightarrow{\rho}(0)
)

\displaystyle\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho},x,a)(0)

\displaystyle:=(k,\rho[x\mapsto a])

\displaystyle\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho},x,a)(1+m)

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\rho}(1+m)

where
\rho[x\mapsto a]
is the same as
\rho
except that when given
x
it returns

a
. We write the iterative applications of this version of
ext
in just one pair of
parentheses:

\displaystyle\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho},x_{1},a_{1},\cdots,x_{i},a_{i}):=\texttt{ext}(\cdots\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho},x_{1},a_{1}),\cdots,x_{i},a_{i})

We use the
drop
operation to drop a binding in the topmost
Env
:

drop

\displaystyle:\textsf{Envs}\to\textsf{Var}\to\textsf{Envs}

(where
(k,\rho):=\overrightarrow{\rho}(0)
and
\rho^{\prime}
is
\rho
with
x

removed from its domain)

\displaystyle\texttt{drop}(\overrightarrow{\rho},x)(0)

\displaystyle:=(k,\rho^{\prime})

\displaystyle\texttt{drop}(\overrightarrow{\rho},x)(1+n)

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\rho}(1+n)

We also define the familiar truncation and truncation offset on
\overrightarrow{\rho}
:

\displaystyle\_\mid{\_}

\displaystyle:\textsf{Envs}\to\mathbb{N}\to\textsf{Envs}

\displaystyle(\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n})(m)

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\rho}(n+m)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\_},{\_})

\displaystyle:\textsf{Envs}\to\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{0})

\displaystyle:=0

(where
\overrightarrow{\rho}(0):=(m,\_)
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{1+n})

\displaystyle:=m+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{1}},{n})

The following lemma holds:

Lemma 6.5.

\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n+m})=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n}},{m})

Proof.

Induction on
n
.
∎

6.3.
Untyped Modal Transformations

To model
\beta
reduction for
\square T
, we shall define untyped modal
transformations (UMoTs),
ranging over
\kappa
, in

D
. We even want to take a step further by considering a unified representation of
compositional closure of modal transformation in
D
. It turns out that
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

is a sufficient representation, which models a unified weakening from an infinite
context stack to another.

We first define operations for this representation of modal transformation:

\displaystyle\_;\Uparrow^{\_}\!\!{}

\displaystyle:(\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N})\to\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle(\kappa;\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{})\;(0)

\displaystyle:=n

\displaystyle(\kappa;\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{})\;(m)

\displaystyle:=\kappa(1+m)

\displaystyle\_\mid{\_}

\displaystyle:(\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N})\to\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle({\kappa\mid{n}})~(m)

\displaystyle:=\kappa(n+m)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\_},{\_})

\displaystyle:(\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N})\to\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{0})

\displaystyle:=0

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{1+n})

\displaystyle:=\kappa(0)+\mathcal{O}({\kappa\mid{1}},{n})

\displaystyle\_\circ\_

\displaystyle:(\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N})\to(\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N})\to\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle(\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime})(0)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{O}({\kappa^{\prime}},{\kappa(0)})

\displaystyle(\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime})(1+n)

\displaystyle:=((\kappa\mid{1})\circ(\kappa^{\prime}\mid{\kappa(0)}))(n)

We also need an identity modal transformation:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{1}

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{1}

\displaystyle:=\_\mapsto 1

We next define application of UMoTs to
D
and
Envs
:

\displaystyle\texttt{box}(a)[\kappa]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{box}(a[\kappa;\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{}])

\displaystyle\Lambda(\overrightarrow{\rho},x,t)[\kappa]

\displaystyle:=\Lambda(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa],x,t)

\displaystyle\uparrow^{T}(c)[\kappa]

\displaystyle:=\uparrow^{T}(c[\kappa])

\displaystyle z[\kappa]

\displaystyle:=z

\displaystyle c\ d[\kappa]

\displaystyle:=(c[\kappa])\ (d[\kappa])

\displaystyle\texttt{unbox}(k,c)[\kappa]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{unbox}(\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{k}),c[\kappa\mid{k}])

\displaystyle\downarrow^{T}(a)[\kappa]

\displaystyle:=\downarrow^{T}(a[\kappa])

(where
(k,\rho):=\overrightarrow{\rho}(0)
)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa](0)

\displaystyle:=(\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{k}),\rho[\kappa])

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa](1+n)

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{1}[\kappa\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{1})}](n)

where
\rho[\kappa]
is defined by mapping
x
to
\rho(x)[\kappa]
if
x
is defined
in
\rho
. Notice that the
1+n
case for
\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]
does not really decrease
by structure; nonetheless, it helps us to understand what
\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]
does. The following lemma
gives a structure-decreasing equation which can be plugged in as the definition, but
it is rather convoluted so we choose to do the otherwise:

Lemma 6.6.

If
\overrightarrow{\rho}(n)=(m,\rho)
, then
\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa](n)=(\mathcal{O}({\kappa\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})}},{m}),\rho[\kappa\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})}])
.

The right hand side might seem unmotivated at the first glance, but it will make more
sense after we establish some lemmas and notice that
\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa](n)=(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]\mid{n})(0)=\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n}[\kappa\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})}](0)
.

We can prove a number of properties for modal transformations.

Lemma 6.7.

The following are properties for
\overrightarrow{1}
:

•

\overrightarrow{1};1=\overrightarrow{1}

•

\overrightarrow{1}\mid{n}=\overrightarrow{1}

•

\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{1}},{n})=n

•

\overrightarrow{1}\circ\kappa=\kappa

•

\kappa\circ\overrightarrow{1}=\kappa

•

a[\overrightarrow{1}]=a

•

c[\overrightarrow{1}]=c

•

d[\overrightarrow{1}]=d

•

\overrightarrow{\rho}[\overrightarrow{1}]=\overrightarrow{\rho}

The following lemmas describe the general interaction between composition and truncation:

Lemma 6.8.

(\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime})\mid{1}=\kappa\mid{1}\circ\kappa^{\prime}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{1})}

Proof.

By definition.
∎

Lemma 6.9.

(\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime})\mid{n}=\kappa\mid{n}\circ\kappa^{\prime}\mid{\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{n})}

Proof.

Do induction on
n
and use the previous lemma.
∎

There is a similar lemma for truncation offset:

Lemma 6.10.

\mathcal{O}({\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime}},{n})=\mathcal{O}({\kappa^{\prime}},{\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{n})})

From this lemma we can derive the associativity:

Lemma 6.11.

(\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime})\circ\kappa^{\prime\prime}=\kappa\circ(\kappa^{\prime}\circ\kappa^{\prime\prime})

We have the following lemma by induction:

Lemma 6.12.

•

a[\kappa][\kappa^{\prime}]=a[\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime}]

•

c[\kappa][\kappa^{\prime}]=c[\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime}]

•

d[\kappa][\kappa^{\prime}]=d[\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime}]

•

\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa][\kappa^{\prime}]=\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime}]

The follow lemmas investigate the interactions between truncation offset, truncation and application. First we
consider the special case where the truncation is
1
.

Lemma 6.13.

\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]\mid{1}=\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{1}[\kappa\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{1})}]

Proof.

Immediate by definition.
∎

We can then generalize the conclusion to all
n
.

Lemma 6.14.

\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]\mid{n}=\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n}[\kappa\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})}]

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
.

Case

n=0
, immediate.

Case

n=1+n^{\prime}
, then we consider its argument
m
:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]\mid{(1+n^{\prime})}(m)

\displaystyle=\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa](1+n^{\prime}+m)

\displaystyle=\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{1}[\kappa\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{1})}](n^{\prime}+m)

\displaystyle=((\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{1}[\kappa\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{1})}])\mid{n^{\prime}})(m)

(by
Lemma

6.13
)

\displaystyle=(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]\mid{1}\mid{n^{\prime}})(m)

\displaystyle=(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]\mid{(1+n^{\prime})})(m)

∎

Lemma 6.15.

\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]},{n})=\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})})

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
.

Case

n=0
, immediate.

Case

n=1+n^{\prime}
, then

(where
(k,\_):=\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa](0)
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]},{n})

\displaystyle=k+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]\mid{1}},{n^{\prime}})

(by
Lemma

6.13
)

\displaystyle=k+\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{1}[\kappa\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{1})}]},{n^{\prime}})

(by IH)

\displaystyle=k+\mathcal{O}({\kappa\mid{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{1})}},{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{1}},{n^{\prime}})})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})})

∎

6.4.
Evaluation

Next we consider the evaluation function, which, given
\overrightarrow{\rho}
, translate an
Exp
to
a
D
.

\displaystyle\llbracket\_\rrbracket

\displaystyle:\textsf{Exp}\to\textsf{Envs}\to D

(where
(\_,\rho):=\overrightarrow{\rho}(0)
)

\displaystyle\llbracket x\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\rho(x)

\displaystyle\llbracket\texttt{box}\ t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\texttt{box}(\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho})))

\displaystyle\llbracket\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\texttt{unbox}\cdot(\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n}),\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n}))

\displaystyle\llbracket\lambda x.t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\Lambda(\overrightarrow{\rho},x,t)

\displaystyle\llbracket t\ s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})\cdot\llbracket s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle\llbracket t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}))

Here we make use of two partial functions which evaluates
box
and
\Lambda
,
respectively.
We define the partial unbox as follows:

\displaystyle\texttt{unbox}\cdot

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}\rightharpoonup D\rightharpoonup D

\displaystyle\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,\texttt{box}(a))

\displaystyle:=a[\overrightarrow{1};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{}]

\displaystyle\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,\uparrow^{\square T}c)

\displaystyle:=\uparrow^{T}(\texttt{unbox}(k,c))

We define the partial application as follows:

\displaystyle\_\cdot\_

\displaystyle:D\rightharpoonup D\rightharpoonup D

\displaystyle(\Lambda(\overrightarrow{\rho},x,t))\cdot a

\displaystyle:=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho},x,a))

\displaystyle(\uparrow^{T\longrightarrow T^{\prime}}c)\cdot a

\displaystyle:=\uparrow^{T^{\prime}}(c\ \downarrow^{T}(a))

In the
t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]
case, we need the interpretation of substitutions:

\displaystyle\llbracket\_\rrbracket

\displaystyle:\textsf{Substs}\to\textsf{Envs}\to\textsf{Envs}

\displaystyle\llbracket\overrightarrow{I}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\rho}

\displaystyle\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma},t/x\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\texttt{ext}(\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}),x,\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}))

\displaystyle\llbracket\textsf{wk}_{x}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\texttt{drop}(\overrightarrow{\rho},x)

\displaystyle\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\texttt{ext}(\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n}),\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n}))

\displaystyle\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}\rrbracket(\llbracket\overrightarrow{\delta}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}))

In the presheaf model shown in
Section

5
, unified weakenings commute with
many operations, e.g. evaluation and exponential application, because of functoriality
and naturality. In the untyped domain, something similar exists.

Lemma 6.16.

\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,a)[\kappa]=\texttt{unbox}\cdot(\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{k}),a[\kappa\mid{k}])

Proof.

We consider the cases where the equation is defined:

Case

a=\texttt{box}(b)
, then

\displaystyle\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,a)[\kappa]

\displaystyle=b[\overrightarrow{1};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{}][\kappa]

\displaystyle=b[\kappa\mid{k};\Uparrow^{\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{k})}\!\!{}]

\displaystyle=b[\kappa\mid{k};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{}][\overrightarrow{1};\Uparrow^{\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{k})}\!\!{}]

\displaystyle=\texttt{unbox}\cdot(\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{k}),b[\kappa\mid{k};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{}])

\displaystyle=\texttt{unbox}\cdot(\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{k}),\texttt{box}(b)[\kappa\mid{k}])

Case

a=\uparrow^{\square T}c
, then

\displaystyle\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,a)[\kappa]

\displaystyle=\uparrow^{T}(\texttt{unbox}(k,c)[\kappa])

\displaystyle=\uparrow^{T}(\texttt{unbox}(\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{k}),c[\kappa\mid{k}]))

\displaystyle=\texttt{unbox}\cdot(\mathcal{O}({\kappa},{k}),(\uparrow^{\square T}c)[\kappa\mid{k}])

∎

In general, lemmas like this should characterize the interaction between untyped modal
transformations and operations.

Lemma 6.17 (Naturality Equalities).

•

\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa])=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa]

•

\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa])=\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa]

•

(a\cdot b)[\kappa]=(a[\kappa])\cdot(b[\kappa])

These three equations including the previous one are collectively called

naturality equalities
. Similar to the presheaf model, we will also need these
equalities to esstablish the completeness proof. Unlike the presheaf model, in which
naturality has been built in as part of various natural transformations, in the
untyped domain model, we have to explicitly list and prove them.

Proof.

These three equalities must be proved mutually. They are proved by analyzing each
defined case and apply IH whenever necessary.
∎

6.5.
Readback Functions

After evaluating an
Exp
to
D
, we have already got the corresponding
\beta

normal form of the term in
D
. We need a one last step to read from
D
back to
normal form and do the
\eta
expansion at the same time to obtain a
\beta\eta

normal form:

\displaystyle\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}

\displaystyle:(\mathbb{N}\rightharpoonup\textsf{Var})\rightharpoonup D^{\textsf{Nf}}\rightharpoonup\textsf{Nf}

\displaystyle\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{\square T}(a))

\displaystyle:=\texttt{box}\ \textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{T}(\texttt{unbox}\cdot(1,a)))

(where
z:=\textsf{next}(\alpha)
)

\displaystyle\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{S\longrightarrow T}(a))

\displaystyle:=\lambda x.\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha[z\mapsto x]}(\downarrow^{T}(a\cdot\uparrow^{S}(z)))

\displaystyle\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{B}(\uparrow^{B}(c)))

\displaystyle:=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c)

\displaystyle\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}

\displaystyle:(\mathbb{N}\rightharpoonup\textsf{Var})\rightharpoonup D^{\textsf{Ne}}\rightharpoonup\textsf{Ne}

(if
\alpha
is undefined at
z
, we assign some fixed default
Var
)

\displaystyle\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(z)

\displaystyle:=\alpha(z)

\displaystyle\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c\ d)

\displaystyle:=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c)\ \textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(d)

\displaystyle\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(\texttt{unbox}(k,c))

\displaystyle:=\texttt{unbox}_{k}\ \textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c)

In the readback function, a function
\alpha
is maintained. It is a function keeping
track of the correspondence between syntactic and domain-level
variables. Conceptually, a domain variable
z
is a natural number remembering the

location
of the syntactic variable in the context stack it corresponds
to. Concretely, there are two solutions:

(1)

z
is the
z
’th variable in its context. That is, if
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:T,\Gamma^{\prime})
where
x
and
z
corresponds, then
z=|\Gamma|
.

(2)

z
is the
z
’th variable in the whole context stack. That is, if
\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:T,\Gamma^{\prime})
where
x
and
z
corresponds, then
z=||\overrightarrow{\Gamma}||+|\Gamma|
, where

\displaystyle||\overrightarrow{\Gamma}||:=\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}|\Gamma^{\prime\prime}|

Either solution would work in our NbE algorithm. The first solution seems to map
multiple syntactic variables to the same natural number, e.g. in the context stack

\epsilon;x:T;y:S
,
\alpha
would map both

x
and
y
to
0
. This is fine because the
0
s representing
x
and
y
are
distinguished by worlds, and variables cannot cross different worlds.

We use
\alpha
to represent the effect from
\mathbb{N}
to
Var
and the opposite direction is

\alpha^{-1}
. Technically speaking, both effects are only defined in finite domains, so
they can only be partial functions, but our logical relations will demonstrate that
invalid inputs are never given to the effects. The
\textsf{next}(\alpha)
function used
in the function case in
\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}
returns the next
available domain-level variable in
\alpha
. If
\alpha
is undefined everywhere, then

\textsf{next}(\alpha)=0
. Otherwise,
\textsf{next}(\alpha)=\max(\text{dom}(\alpha))+1
. We will discuss how

\alpha
is implemented when de Bruijn indices are used in the syntactic level in
Section

6.11
.

We define the initial
evaluation environment:

\displaystyle\uparrow^{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle:\textsf{Envs}

\displaystyle\uparrow^{\epsilon;\Gamma}

\displaystyle:=\texttt{ext}(\textsf{empty},x\mapsto\uparrow^{T}(\alpha^{-1}(x))\text{
if }x:T\in\Gamma)

\displaystyle\uparrow^{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma}

\displaystyle:=\texttt{ext}(\uparrow^{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}},x\mapsto\uparrow^{T}(\alpha^{-1}(x))\text{
if }x:T\in\Gamma)

where
\alpha^{-1}:\textsf{Var}\to\mathbb{N}
is the opposite of
\alpha
.

Definition 6.18.

The NbE algorithm is defined to be

\displaystyle\textsf{nbe}_{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}^{T}(t):=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{T}(\llbracket t\rrbracket(\uparrow^{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}})))

where
\alpha
is defined as above.

6.6.
Candidate Space and PER Model

We define two PERs over
D
from which we form a candidate space for each type. For a PER over

D
,
P
, we write
a\approx b\in P
for
(a,b)\in P
:

\frac{\forall\alpha,\kappa.\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{T}(a[\kappa]))=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{T}(b[\kappa]))}{a\approx b\in\top^{T}}\par\frac{\forall\alpha,\kappa.\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c[\kappa])=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c^{\prime}[\kappa])}{\uparrow^{T}(c)\approx\uparrow^{T}(c^{\prime})\in\bot^{T}}

\bot^{T}
and
\top^{T}
are binary relations over
D
. Furthermore, we can easily verify
that they are partial equivalence relations (PERs). Note that the universal
quantification over
\alpha
here still maintain a correspondence.

We then interpret the types to a PER model:

\displaystyle\llbracket B\rrbracket

\displaystyle:=\bot^{B}

\displaystyle\llbracket\square T\rrbracket

\displaystyle:=\{(a,b)\;|\;\forall k,\kappa.\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,a[\kappa])\approx\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,b[\kappa])\in\llbracket T\rrbracket\}

\displaystyle\llbracket S\longrightarrow T\rrbracket

\displaystyle:=\{(a,b)\;|\;\forall\kappa,a^{\prime}\approx b^{\prime}\in\llbracket S\rrbracket.a[\kappa]\cdot a^{\prime}\approx b[\kappa]\cdot b^{\prime}\in\llbracket T\rrbracket\}

Next we demonstrate an important property of the PER model, realizability.
Realizability of
\llbracket T\rrbracket
states
\bot_{T}\subseteq\llbracket T\rrbracket\subseteq\top_{T}
. The
realizability theorem states that all
\llbracket T\rrbracket
are realizable.

We first need the following lemma:

Lemma 6.19.

\uparrow^{T}(z)\approx\uparrow^{T}(z)\in\bot^{T}

Proof.

Holds by definition.
∎

6.6.1.
Realizability

Theorem 6.20 (Realizability).

For all type
T
,
\bot^{T}\subseteq\llbracket T\rrbracket\subseteq\top^{T}
.

Proof.

We do induction on
T
.

Case

T=B
, immediate.

Case

T=\square T^{\prime}
,

Subcase

We shall show
\bot^{\square T^{\prime}}\subseteq\llbracket\square T^{\prime}\rrbracket
. Our goal is
to apply
\bot^{T^{\prime}}\subseteq\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket
as IH and thus we will first need to
construct some terms related by
\bot^{T^{\prime}}
.

(assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\ \forall\alpha,\kappa.\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c[\kappa])=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c^{\prime}[\kappa])

\displaystyle\ \text{assume }k,\kappa,\alpha,\kappa^{\prime}

(by
H_{1}
)

\displaystyle\ \textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c[\kappa\circ(\kappa^{\prime}\mid{k})])=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c^{\prime}[\kappa\circ(\kappa^{\prime}\mid{k})])

(by congruence)

\displaystyle\ \textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(\texttt{unbox}(\mathcal{O}({\kappa^{\prime}},{k}),c[\kappa][\kappa^{\prime}\mid{k}]))=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(\texttt{unbox}(\mathcal{O}({\kappa^{\prime}},{k}),c^{\prime}[\kappa][\kappa^{\prime}\mid{k}]))

(by definition)

\displaystyle\ \textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(\texttt{unbox}(k,c[\kappa])[\kappa^{\prime}])=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(\texttt{unbox}(k,c^{\prime}[\kappa])[\kappa^{\prime}])

(by abstraction)

\displaystyle\ \uparrow^{T^{\prime}}(\texttt{unbox}(k,c[\kappa]))\approx\uparrow^{T^{\prime}}(\texttt{unbox}(k,c^{\prime}[\kappa]))\in\bot^{T^{\prime}}

(by IH)

\displaystyle\ \uparrow^{T^{\prime}}(\texttt{unbox}(k,c[\kappa]))\approx\uparrow^{T^{\prime}}(\texttt{unbox}(k,c^{\prime}[\kappa]))\in\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket

(by abstraction)

\displaystyle\ \uparrow^{\square T^{\prime}}(c)\approx\uparrow^{\square T^{\prime}}(c)\in\llbracket\square T^{\prime}\rrbracket

Subcase

Now we show
\llbracket\square T^{\prime}\rrbracket\subseteq\top^{\square T^{\prime}}
. Assuming

a,b:D
,
\alpha
and
\kappa
, then we shall prove

\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{\square T^{\prime}}(a[\kappa]))=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{\square T^{\prime}}(b[\kappa]))
:

(assumption)

\displaystyle\forall k,\kappa.\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,a[\kappa])\approx\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,b[\kappa])\in\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\texttt{unbox}\cdot(1,a[\kappa])\approx\texttt{unbox}\cdot(1,b[\kappa])\in\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket

(by IH)

\displaystyle\texttt{unbox}\cdot(1,a[\kappa])\approx\texttt{unbox}\cdot(1,b[\kappa])\in\top^{T^{\prime}}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{T^{\prime}}(\texttt{unbox}\cdot(1,a[\kappa])))=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{T^{\prime}}(\texttt{unbox}\cdot(1,b[\kappa])))

(by congruence)

\displaystyle\texttt{box}\ \textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{T^{\prime}}(\texttt{unbox}\cdot(1,a[\kappa])))=\texttt{box}\ \textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{T^{\prime}}(\texttt{unbox}\cdot(1,b[\kappa])))

\displaystyle\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{\square T^{\prime}}(a[\kappa]))=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha}(\downarrow^{\square T^{\prime}}(b[\kappa]))

Case

T=S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}
, then

Subcase

\bot^{S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}}\subseteq\llbracket S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}\rrbracket
is rather straightforward.

(assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\ \forall\alpha,\kappa.\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c[\kappa])=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c^{\prime}[\kappa])

\displaystyle\ \text{assume }\kappa^{\prime},a\approx b\in\llbracket S\rrbracket

(by IH)

\displaystyle\ a\approx b\in\top^{S}

(by
H_{2}
)

\displaystyle\ \forall\alpha,\kappa.\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c[\kappa^{\prime}\circ\kappa])=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}(c^{\prime}[\kappa^{\prime}\circ\kappa])

\displaystyle\ \forall\alpha,\kappa.\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}((c[\kappa^{\prime}]\ \downarrow^{S}(a))[\kappa])=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Ne}}_{\alpha}((c^{\prime}[\kappa]\ \downarrow^{S}(b))[\kappa])

(by definition)

\displaystyle\ \uparrow^{T^{\prime}}(c[\kappa^{\prime}]\ \downarrow^{S}(a))\approx\uparrow^{T^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}[\kappa^{\prime}]\ \downarrow^{S}(b))\in\bot^{T^{\prime}}

(by IH)

\displaystyle\ \uparrow^{T^{\prime}}(c[\kappa^{\prime}]\ \downarrow^{S}(a))\approx\uparrow^{T^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}[\kappa^{\prime}]\ \downarrow^{S}(b))\in\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket

(by abstraction)

\displaystyle\ \uparrow^{S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}}(c)\approx\uparrow^{S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}}(c^{\prime})\in\llbracket S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}\rrbracket

We will only show one of the two equations as they are symmetric. We assume

\kappa^{\prime\prime}
, then

\displaystyle\uparrow^{S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}}(c)[\kappa^{\prime}][\kappa^{\prime\prime}]\cdot a[\kappa^{\prime\prime}]

\displaystyle=\uparrow^{T^{\prime}}(c[\kappa^{\prime}][\kappa^{\prime\prime}]\ \downarrow^{S}(a[\kappa^{\prime\prime}]))

\displaystyle=\uparrow^{T^{\prime}}(c[\kappa^{\prime}]\ \downarrow^{S}(a))[\kappa^{\prime\prime}]

\displaystyle=(\uparrow^{S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}}(c[\kappa^{\prime}])\cdot a)[\kappa^{\prime\prime}]

Thus they agree.

Subcase

\llbracket S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}\rrbracket\subseteq\top^{S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}}
, assume
a\approx b\in\llbracket S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}\rrbracket
,
\alpha
and
\kappa
, then

(by
Lemma

6.19
)

\displaystyle\uparrow^{S}(z)\approx\uparrow^{S}(z)\in\bot^{S}

(by IH)

\displaystyle\uparrow^{S}(z)\approx\uparrow^{S}(z)\in\llbracket S\rrbracket

\displaystyle a[\kappa]\cdot\uparrow^{S}(z)\approx b[\kappa]\cdot\uparrow^{S}(z)\in\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket

(by IH)

\displaystyle a[\kappa]\cdot\uparrow^{S}(z)\approx b[\kappa]\cdot\uparrow^{S}(z)\in\top^{T^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha[z\mapsto x]}\downarrow^{T}(a[\kappa]\cdot\uparrow^{S}(z))=\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha[z\mapsto x]}\downarrow^{T}(b[\kappa]\cdot\uparrow^{S}(z))

(by congruence)

\displaystyle\lambda x.\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha[z\mapsto x]}\downarrow^{T}(a[\kappa]\cdot\uparrow^{S}(z))=\lambda x.\textsf{R}^{\textsf{Nf}}_{\alpha[z\mapsto x]}\downarrow^{T}(b[\kappa]\cdot\uparrow^{S}(z))

(by abstraction)

\displaystyle a\approx b\in\top^{S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}}

∎

Lemma 6.21.

\llbracket T\rrbracket
is a PER.

Next, we generalize the PER model to both contexts and context stacks.

\displaystyle\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket

\displaystyle:=\{(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\;|\;\rho(x)\approx\rho^{\prime}(x)\in\llbracket T\rrbracket\text{ if }x:T\in\Gamma\}

\displaystyle\llbracket\epsilon;\Gamma\rrbracket

\displaystyle:=\{(\overrightarrow{\rho},\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})\;|\;\rho\approx\rho\in\Gamma\text{ where }(k,\rho):=\overrightarrow{\rho}(0)\text{ and }(k^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}):=\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}(0)\}

\displaystyle\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\rrbracket

\displaystyle:=\{(\overrightarrow{\rho},\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})\;|\;k=k^{\prime}\text{ and }\rho\approx\rho\in\Gamma\text{ and }\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{1}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\mid{1}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket\text{ where }(k,\rho):=\overrightarrow{\rho}(0)\text{ and }(k^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}):=\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}(0)\}

Following lemma holds:

Lemma 6.22.

If
\overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket
, then
\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}},{n})
.

Lemma 6.23.

If
\overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket
, then
\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\mid{n}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}|_{n}\rrbracket
.

Lemma 6.24.

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket
are PERs.

We define the semantic judgments:

Definition 6.25.

We define semantic typing and semantic typing equivalence as follows:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t\approx t^{\prime}:T

\displaystyle:=\forall\overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket.\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})\approx\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})\in\llbracket T\rrbracket

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\text{ and }\forall\kappa.\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa])=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa]\text{ and }\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}[\kappa])=\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})[\kappa]

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}

\displaystyle:=\forall\overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket.\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})\approx\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Delta}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\text{ and }\forall\kappa.\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa])=\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa]\text{ and }\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}[\kappa])=\llbracket\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})[\kappa]

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t:T

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t\approx t:T

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}

Notice that we add
in the semantic judgment, so that we can make
sure the conjecture holds for the well-typed terms. We call these equations
naturality equalities
.

6.6.2.
Kripke Structure of PER Model

It turns out that the PER model
\llbracket T\rrbracket
is closed under modal transformations. In
fact, we can view
\llbracket T\rrbracket
as a Kripke relation where the wolds are connected by
modal transformations. This intuition is justified by the following lemma.

Lemma 6.26.

If
a\approx b\in\llbracket T\rrbracket
, then
a[\kappa]\approx b[\kappa]\in\llbracket T\rrbracket
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
T
.

Case

T=B
, immediate because
\kappa
is closed under composition.

Case

T=\square T^{\prime}
, assume
\kappa
, then

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\ \forall k,\kappa^{\prime}.\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,a[\kappa^{\prime}])\approx\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,b[\kappa^{\prime}])\in\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\ \text{assume }k,\kappa^{\prime}

(by
H_{1}
)

\displaystyle\ (\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,a[\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime}]))\approx(\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,b[\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime}]))\in\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket

(by definition)

\displaystyle\ (\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,a[\kappa][\kappa^{\prime}]))\approx(\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,b[\kappa][\kappa^{\prime}]))\in\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket

(by abstraction)

\displaystyle\ a[\kappa]\approx b[\kappa]\in\llbracket\square T^{\prime}\rrbracket

Case

T=S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}
, assume
\kappa
, then

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\ \forall\kappa^{\prime},a^{\prime}\approx b^{\prime}\in\llbracket S\rrbracket.a[\kappa^{\prime}]\cdot a^{\prime}\approx b[\kappa^{\prime}]\cdot b^{\prime}\in\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\ \text{assume }\kappa^{\prime},a^{\prime}\approx b^{\prime}\in\llbracket S\rrbracket

(by
H_{2}
with
\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\ a[\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime}]\cdot a^{\prime}\approx b[\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime}]\cdot b^{\prime}\in\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\ a[\kappa][\kappa^{\prime}]\cdot a^{\prime}\approx b[\kappa][\kappa^{\prime}]\cdot b^{\prime}\in\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket

(by abstraction)

\displaystyle\ a[\kappa]\approx b[\kappa]\in\llbracket S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}\rrbracket

∎

This lemma holds because untyped modal transformations are closed under composition.

We can generalize this lemma of types to context stacks:

Lemma 6.27.

If
\overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket
, then
\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}[\kappa]\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket
.

6.7.
Completeness

In this section, we will cover the semantic equivalence typing rules and the
fundamental theorem which justifies the the completeness theorem.

6.7.1.
PER Rules

Lemma 6.28.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash s\approx t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t\approx s:T}

Lemma 6.29.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash s\approx t:T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t\approx u:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash s\approx u:T}

Proof.

We use the fact that
\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket T\rrbracket
are PER.
∎

6.7.2.
Congruence Rules

Lemma 6.30.

\frac{x:T\in\Gamma}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vDash x\approx x:T}

Proof.

Immediate because we are doing the same lookup.
∎

Lemma 6.31.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\vDash t\approx t^{\prime}:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\texttt{box}\ t\approx\texttt{box}\ t^{\prime}:\square T}

Proof.

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\vDash t\approx t^{\prime}:T

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket

(by definition)

\displaystyle H_{3}:

\displaystyle\ \texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho})\approx\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\rrbracket

(by
H_{1}
and
H_{3}
)

\displaystyle\ \llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}))\approx\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}))\in\llbracket T\rrbracket

\displaystyle\ \text{assume }k,\kappa

(by
Lemma

6.26
)

\displaystyle\ \llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}))[\kappa;\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{}][\overrightarrow{1};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{}]\approx\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}))[\kappa;\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{}][\overrightarrow{1};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{}]\in\llbracket T\rrbracket

(by abstraction)

\displaystyle\ \forall k,\kappa.\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,\texttt{box}(\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho})))[\kappa])\approx\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,\texttt{box}(\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})))[\kappa])\in\llbracket T\rrbracket

(by definition)

\displaystyle\ \llbracket\texttt{box}\ t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})\approx\llbracket\texttt{box}\ t^{\prime}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})\in\llbracket\square T\rrbracket

∎

Lemma 6.32.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:S)\vDash t\approx t^{\prime}:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vDash\lambda x.t\approx\lambda x.t^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T}

Proof.

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:S)\vDash t\approx t^{\prime}:T

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\rrbracket

\displaystyle\ \text{assume }\kappa,a\approx a^{\prime}\in\llbracket S\rrbracket

(by
Lemma

6.27
)

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa]\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}[\kappa]\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\rrbracket

(by definition)

\displaystyle H_{3}:

\displaystyle\ \texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa],x,a)\approx\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}[\kappa],x,a^{\prime})\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:S)\rrbracket

(by
H_{1}
and
H_{3}
)

\displaystyle\ \llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa],x,a))\approx\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}[\kappa],x,a^{\prime}))\in\llbracket T\rrbracket

\displaystyle H_{4}:

\displaystyle\ \forall\kappa^{\prime}.\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa],x,a)[\kappa^{\prime}])=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa],x,a))[\kappa^{\prime}]

(by
H_{1}
and
H_{3}
)

\displaystyle\ \text{ and }\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}[\kappa],x,a^{\prime})[\kappa^{\prime}])=\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}[\kappa],x,a^{\prime}))[\kappa^{\prime}]

(by definition)

\displaystyle\ \Lambda(\overrightarrow{\rho},x,t)[\kappa]\cdot a\approx\Lambda(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime},x,t^{\prime})[\kappa]\cdot a^{\prime}\in\llbracket T\rrbracket

(by abstraction)

\displaystyle\ \llbracket\lambda x.t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})\approx\llbracket\lambda x.t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})\in\llbracket S\longrightarrow T\rrbracket

∎

Lemma 6.33.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t\approx t^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash s\approx s^{\prime}:S}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t\ s\approx t^{\prime}\ s^{\prime}:T}

Proof.

Assuming
\overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\rrbracket
, we demonstrate that

\forall\kappa.\llbracket t\ s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa^{\prime}])=\llbracket t\ s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa]
. Assuming

\kappa
, we compute:

\displaystyle\llbracket t\ s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa^{\prime}])

\displaystyle=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa^{\prime}])\cdot\llbracket s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}[\kappa^{\prime}])

\displaystyle=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa^{\prime}]\cdot\llbracket s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa^{\prime}]

and

\displaystyle\llbracket t\ s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa]

\displaystyle=(\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})\cdot\llbracket s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}))[\kappa]

Now we need to equate both sides, and that is exactly provided by
\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})\approx\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})\in\llbracket S\longrightarrow T\rrbracket
.
∎

All following are immediate just by expanding the definitions:

Lemma 6.34.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t\approx t^{\prime}:\square T\quad|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|=n}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vDash\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t\approx\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ t^{\prime}:T^{\prime}}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vDash t\approx t^{\prime}:T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]\approx t^{\prime}[\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}]:T}\par\frac{}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{I}\approx\overrightarrow{I}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\Gamma\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t\approx t^{\prime}:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma},x:t\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime},x:t^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};(\Gamma,x:T)}\par\frac{}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:T)\vDash\textsf{wk}_{x}\approx\textsf{wk}_{x}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\quad|\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}|=n}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{}:\overrightarrow{\Delta};\cdot}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\delta}\approx\overrightarrow{\delta}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}\approx\overrightarrow{\sigma}^{\prime}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}

Proof.

Immediate by premises.
∎

6.7.3.
\beta
Rules

Lemma 6.35.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\vDash t:T\quad|\overrightarrow{\Delta}|=n}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vDash\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ (\texttt{box}\ t)\approx t[\overrightarrow{I};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{}]:T}

Proof.

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma};\cdot\vDash t:T

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\rrbracket

We compute both sides:

\displaystyle\llbracket\texttt{unbox}_{n}\ (\texttt{box}\ t)\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle=\texttt{unbox}\cdot(\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n}),\texttt{box}(\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n}))))

\displaystyle=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n}))[\overrightarrow{1};\Uparrow^{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})}\!\!{}]

(by naturality equalities)

\displaystyle=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n}))[\overrightarrow{1};\Uparrow^{\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})}\!\!{}])

(by definition)

\displaystyle=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n},\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})))

\displaystyle\llbracket t[\overrightarrow{I};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{}]\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\llbracket\overrightarrow{I};\Uparrow^{n}\!\!{}\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\mid{n},\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}},{n})))

We also know

(by
H_{2}
)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\mid{n}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket

(by
H_{2}
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n})=\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}},{n})

\displaystyle\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}\mid{n},\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}},{n}))\approx\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\mid{n},\mathcal{O}({\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}},{n}))\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\overrightarrow{\Delta}\rrbracket

Thus the target holds by
H_{1}
.
∎

Lemma 6.36.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:S)\vDash t:T\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vDash s:S}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vDash(\lambda x.t)\ s\approx t[\overrightarrow{I},s/x]:T}

Proof.

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:S)\vDash t:T

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vDash s:S

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{3}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\rrbracket

We compute both sides:

\displaystyle\llbracket(\lambda x.t)\ s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})

\displaystyle=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho},x,\llbracket s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})))

\displaystyle\llbracket t[\overrightarrow{I},s/x]\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\llbracket\overrightarrow{I},s/x\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime},x,\llbracket s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})))

We follow the definition and can show

\displaystyle\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho},x,\llbracket s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}))\approx\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime},x,\llbracket s\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}))\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:S)\rrbracket

The target immediately follows.
∎

6.7.4.
\eta
Rules

Lemma 6.37.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t:\square T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t\approx\texttt{box}\ \texttt{unbox}_{1}\ t:\square T}

Proof.

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t:\square T

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket

(by
H_{1}
)

\displaystyle H_{3}:

\displaystyle\ \llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})\approx\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})\in\llbracket\square T\rrbracket

Since

\displaystyle\llbracket\texttt{box}\ (\texttt{unbox}_{1}\ t)\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\texttt{box}(\texttt{unbox}\cdot(1,\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})))

we shall prove

\displaystyle\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})\approx\texttt{box}(\texttt{unbox}\cdot(1,\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})))\in\llbracket\square T\rrbracket

We proceed by its definition. Assuming
k
and
\kappa
, we need to prove

\displaystyle\ \texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa])\approx(\texttt{unbox}\cdot(1,\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})))[\kappa;\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{}][\overrightarrow{1};\Uparrow^{k}\!\!{}]\in\llbracket T\rrbracket

(by
Lemma

6.16
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\ \texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa])\approx(\texttt{unbox}\cdot(k,\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})[\kappa]))\in\llbracket T\rrbracket

but this holds by
H_{3}
.
∎

Lemma 6.38.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t:S\longrightarrow T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t\approx\lambda x.(t[\textsf{wk}_{x}]\ x):S\longrightarrow T}

Proof.

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t:S\longrightarrow T

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket

(by
H_{1}
)

\displaystyle H_{3}:

\displaystyle\ \llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})\approx\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})\in\llbracket S\longrightarrow T\rrbracket

We reason about the goal, and we shall prove

\displaystyle\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})\approx\Lambda(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime},x,t[\textsf{wk}_{x}]\ x)\in\llbracket S\longrightarrow T\rrbracket

We proceed by its definition. Assuming
\kappa
,
a\approx b\in\llbracket S\rrbracket
, then
we have the goal as

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\ \llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa]\cdot a\approx\llbracket t\rrbracket(\texttt{drop}(\texttt{ext}(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}[\kappa],x,b),x))\cdot b\in\llbracket S\longrightarrow T\rrbracket

(by definition)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\ \llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa]\cdot a\approx\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}[\kappa])\cdot b\in\llbracket S\longrightarrow T\rrbracket

(by naturality equalities)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\ \llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho})[\kappa]\cdot a\approx\llbracket t\rrbracket(\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime})[\kappa]\cdot b\in\llbracket S\longrightarrow T\rrbracket

We can conclude the last by
H_{3}
.
∎

6.7.5.
Substitution Application Rules

The following rules are immediate by following the definitions:

Lemma 6.39.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t[\overrightarrow{I}]\approx t:T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\delta}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime}\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}\vDash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\overrightarrow{\delta}]\approx t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}][\overrightarrow{\delta}]:T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\Gamma\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash x[\overrightarrow{\sigma},t/x]\approx t:T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Gamma}^{\prime};\Gamma\quad\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash t:T^{\prime}\quad y:T\in\Gamma}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash y[\overrightarrow{\sigma},t/x]\approx y[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]:T}\par\frac{y:T\in\Gamma}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};(\Gamma,x:T^{\prime})\vDash y[\textsf{wk}_{x}]\approx y:T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta};\Delta\quad\overrightarrow{\Delta};\Delta,x:S\vDash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma};\Gamma\vDash\lambda x.t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]\approx\lambda x.(t[(\overrightarrow{\sigma}\circ\textsf{wk}_{x}),x/x]):S\longrightarrow T}\par\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\quad\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vDash s:S\longrightarrow T\quad\overrightarrow{\Delta}\vDash t:S}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash s\ t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]\approx(s[\overrightarrow{\sigma}])\ (t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]):T}

Proof.

Immediate by definitions.
∎

Lemma 6.40.

\frac{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}\quad\overrightarrow{\Delta};\cdot\vDash t:T}{\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\texttt{box}\ t[\overrightarrow{\sigma}]\approx\texttt{box}\ (t[\overrightarrow{\sigma};\Uparrow^{1}\!\!{}]):\square T}

Proof.

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Gamma}\vDash\overrightarrow{\sigma}:\overrightarrow{\Delta}

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\Delta};\cdot\vDash t:T

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{3}:

\displaystyle\ \overrightarrow{\rho}\approx\overrightarrow{\rho}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\overrightarrow{\Gamma}\rrbracket

We compute both sides:
